Ἅμα ταῦτα τῷ βασιλεῖ διηγγέλλετο, καὶ ὁ τῶν ἀνακτόρων αὐτομάτως κατέπιπτε πύργος, καθαπερεὶ διὰ τῶν ἀψύχων τὸν ἀλαζόνα τοῦ Θεοῦ παιδεύειν ἐθέλοντος. Τίνα δὲ καὶ προσιόντι τῷ ὁσίῳ τὰ θαύματα (ὥσπερ γὰρ τῆς πόλεως μετὰ τούτων ἐξῄει, οὕτω καὶ εἰσιόντι πάλιν αὐτὰ συνείπετο οἷα καὶ αὐτῆς φάναι τῆς τοῦ Θεοῦ δυνάμεως τὸν θεραπεύοντα προπεμπούσης), ἐκ τούτων ὀλίγα διὰ τὸν κόρον ὡς ἐν παραδρομῇ θεὶς ἐπὶ τὰ λειπόμενα μετελεύσομαι.
ΜΖ΄. Δύο μὲν αὐτῷ νεανίσκοι τὸ πρῶτον ἰῶνται δαίμοσιν ἐνοχλούμενοι · ἔπειτα γυνή τις ἄλλη Χαναναία τὴν πίστιν, θυγάτριον ἔχουσα καὶ οἷα μάρτυρας τῆς φλεγμαινούσης ψυχῆς τὰ τῶν ὀφθαλμῶν προχέουσα δάκρυα, καὶ δι᾿ αὐτῶν ζητοῦσα τὴν θεραπείαν, ὑγιᾶ μὲν τὴν παῖδα λαμβάνει, χαρὰν δὲ ἀντὶ λύπης κομισαμένη οἴκαδε ἐπανήκει. Εἶτα καὶ διὰ τῆς πόλεως μέσης τῷ ὁσίῳ χωροῦντι τῶν τῆς βουλῆς τις πατρικίων τὰ Ζαχχαίου καὶ αὐτὸς μιμησάμενος καὶ τῷ ἰδίῳ οἴκῳ κατὰ θερμὴν τὴν πίστιν εἰσοικισάμενος, τῆς ὁμοίας ἔτυχεν εὐλογίας. Ἐπεὶ δὲ καὶ πρὸς αὐτῷ ἤδη τῷ μεγίστῳ τοῦ Θεοῦ ναῷ ἦν, καὶ τῷ πατριάρχῃ συντῇ μετὰ πολλῆς αὐτὸν δεξαμένῳ τῆς ἡδονῆς ὁμοῦ καὶ τιμῆς, κἀνταῦθα τοῖς μὲν ἄλλοις καινά, ἑαυτῷ δὲ συνήθη θαυματουργεῖ. Ὄφις ποθὲν ἑρπύσας καὶ τοῖς τοῦ μεγάλου ποσὶ συσπειραθεὶς φοβερὸν θέαμα ἦν · καὶ πρὸς ἄμυναν τῶν παρόντων ἐπιδραμόντων ἐκεῖνοις μὲν τὴν δειλίαν κατονειδίζει, αὐτὸς δὲ τῷ ὄφει θαρρῦντως ἀπειλησάμενος καὶ ὡς ὑπηρέτῃ κατὰ πολλὴν αὐθεντίαν ἐγκελευσάμενος, Πορεύου, φησί, καὶ φωλεοῦ τοῦ συνήθους ἔχου καὶ τῶν ὑπὸ γῆν καταδύσεων. Ὁ δὲ ὥσπερ μὴ ἀντιλέγειν δυνάμενος ἕρπει ἐς πρὸς τὸν τοῖχον αὐτίκα καὶ ὑπὸ τοῖς πάντων ὀφθαλμοῖς διαῤῥήγνυται.
ΜΗ΄. Ταῦτα καὶ Ῥαὶς περὶ τοῦ μεγάλου μαθοῦσα, γυνὴ τὴν μὲν πίστιν τῶν πάνυ θερμοτάτων καὶ εὐσεβῶν, τὴν δὲ δόξαν τῶν ἄγαν ἐπιφανῶν, προθύμως τε συνδραμοῦσα καὶ παραπλησίως τῇ πόρνῃ τοὺς ἐκείνου πόδας δάκρυσι βρέχουσα, παρεκάλει δι᾿ εὐχῆς αὐτῇ γενέσθαι υἱόν. Ὡς δὲ κάτω κειμένη τοῦ ποδὸς αὐτῷ τὸν ταρὸν ἐκκεκομμένον τῇ ἀσκήσει τοῦ τένοντος ἐθεάσατο, καταπλήττεται τὴν ὑπομονήν, καὶ φωνὰς τῇ Χαναναίᾳ παραπλησίας ἀφίησι. Διδοῦσα γὰρ σπαρτίον ἠξίου τῷ ποδὶ ἐκείνῳ περιελίξαντα πάλιν αὐτῇ δοῦναι τοῦτο βοῶσα πλήρωμα τῆς αἰτήσεως. Ὁ δὲ πρῶτον μὲν οὐκ ἠνείχετο · ἔπειτα καὶ τοῖς ἐκείνης δυσπωτηθεὶς δάκρυσι, πρὸς δὲ καὶ τοῖς τοῦ ἐπισκόπου καὶ τῶν λοιπῶν καμφθεὶς ῥήμασιν, οὐ μόνον αὐτῷ σπαρτίον περιελίξας δέδωκεν, ἀλλὰ καὶ παῖδεν αὐτὴν τεκεῖν προεσήμανε, καὶ τὴν τοῦ


[1029] {#pg.116.1029}
παιδὸς κλῆσιν προανεφώνησε, Ζήνωνα, φάσκων, ὦ γύναι, τὸν παῖδα καλέσεις. Καὶ οὕτω μὲν ὁ ἅγιος. Ἐγὼ δὲ οὐδὲν ἄρτι πλέον ἐρῶ· δῆλον γὰρ ὅτι γέγονεν· ἐπεὶ μηδὲν διέπιπτε τῶν ὑπ᾿ ἐκείνου προλεγομένων. Διὰ ταῦτα καὶ τὸν ὅσιον ὁ βασιλεὺς δεδοικὼς, καὶ τὸ πρὸς αὐτὸν ἀντιπνέειν ὅλως ἐκκλίνων τέχνῃ καὶ κολακείᾳ τοῦτον μετελθεῖν ἐπεχείρει· καὶ ἐπειδὴ πολλάκις αὐτὸν παραγενέσθαι καὶ διὰ πολλῶν δειθεὶς οὐκ ἔπειθεν, αὐτὸς πρὸς αὐτὸν ἦλθε, καὶ ἐν οἰκέτου σχήματι προσκυνεῖ, καὶ ποδῶν ἅπτεται τῶν ἐκείνου, συγγνώμης δῆθεν ἀξιωθῆναι δεόμενος. Ὁ δὲ (καὶ τίς γὰρ ἐκείνου διορῶν ταῦτα ὀξυωπέστερος ;) ἐκεῖνον μὲν τότε πολλοῖς τοῖς ὀνείδεσι πλύνει· Ὕστερον δὲ καὶ τὸ τοὺς πολλοὺς λανθάνον ἀνακαλύπτει, Τὸ ταπεινὸν ἐκεῖνο καὶ συντετριμμένον, εἰπὼν, ὑπόκρισις καὶ μηχάνημα ἦν, ὑπὸ δασέϊ κωδίῳ τὸ χαλεπὸν αὐτοῦ καὶ ἄγριον συγκαλύπτων. Ἀλλὰ διὰ τάχους ὄψεται τὸν πάντα διορῶντα τοῦ Θεοῦ ὀφθαλμὸν καὶ τὴν κραταιὰν χεῖρα καὶ τοὺς δυνατοὺς καταβάλλουσαν. Ὁ μακάριος τοσαῦτα θαυματουργήσας καὶ τὸν τοῦ Θεοῦ πολέμιον τρέψας, καὶ τὰ τῆς Ἐκκλησίας εὐκαταστήσας, ἀλλὰ δὴ καὶ προφητεύσας τὰ μέλλοντα, πάλιν ἐπὶ τὸν στῦλον γίνεται, καὶ τῶν ἀγώνων τε καὶ πόνων εὐψύχως ἀντιλαμβάνεται.
ΜΘ΄. Οὐ πολὺς ἐν μέσῳ χρόνος, καὶ Βασιλίσκος μὲν, ὡς ἡ τοῦ Δανιὴλ πρόρρησις, τῶν βασιλείων ἐκβάλλεται, Ζήνων δὲ πάλιν τῶν σκήπτρων τε περιέρχεται, καὶ αὐτῷ τὰ τῶν προρρήσεων πέρας ἐλάμβανε, πρὸς τὸν μέγαν τε καὶ μετὰ τῆς γυναικὸς ἄνεισι καταλλήλους μὲν τῶν προρρήσεων τὰς ἐκβάσεις ἰδὼν, ἀξίας δὲ τῶν ἐκβάσεων ἀποδίδωσιν εὐχαριστίας. Ἤδη δὲ τοῦ λόγου τὸ τέλος ζητοῦντος καὶ τῷ τοῦ μεγάλου βίῳ συναναπαυθῆναι προθυμουμένου, μικρὸν ἔτι τοῖς ἐκείνου θαύμασιν ἐντρυφήσωμεν. Ἀνὴρ τις χρυσοχόος· τὴν τέχνην παίδιον ἔσχεν ἐδδόμου μὲν ἤδη τοῦ ἔτους ἁπτόμενον, οὔπω δὲ καὶ βαδίζειν δυνάμενον, ἀλλὰ καὶ τὰ ἑρπυστικὰ τῶν ζώων ἐν τῷ κινεῖσθαι τῇ κοιλίᾳ χρώμενον. Τοῦτον οἱ τοκεῖς λαβόντες προτιθοῦσι τοῦ στύλου, καὶ αὐτοὶ κάτωθεν οἰκτρὸν τι καὶ δακρυῶδες ἐπικυκλῶντες καὶ αὐτοὺς τοὺς ὁρῶντας ἐξεκαλοῦντο πρὸς δάκρυα. Ὁ δὲ ὅσιος ἄνωθεν διακύψας, καὶ τοῦτο μὲν ἐπιτιμήσει, τοῦτο δὲ καὶ παραινέσει τὸ πένθος αὐτοῖς στήσας, τῷ ἱερῷ προσκαρτερεῖν κελεύει τῷ Συμεὼν οἴκῳ, καὶ τοῖς σεπτοῖς χρῆσθαι λειψάνοις, καὶ οἷόν τι λυτήριον ταῦτα τοῖς ποσὶ τούς· νόσῳ πεδεδημένοις ἐπιτιθέναι. Εἶπε, καὶ γέγονε. Καὶ κατὰ τὴν ἑβδόμην εὐχῆς ἃ ἔθος ὑπὸ τοῦ μάκαρος τελουμένης (ὦ θαυμασίων ἔργων!) δημιουργὲ Κύριε, ἁλλόμενον εὐθὺς τὸ παιδίον ὁρῶσι τῶν κιόνων τε τοὺς βαθμοὺς ἀναβαῖνον καὶ τῷ στύλῳ περιπλεκόμενον.
Ν΄. Ἕτερός τις ἐκ τῆς ἀνατολῆς τὴν πορείαν ποιούμενος περιπίπτει λῃσταῖς. Οἱ δὲ πληγὰς αὐτῷ, κατὰ τοὺς ἱεροὺς λόγους εἰπεῖν, ἐπιθέντες καὶ λαβόντες ἃ ἐπεφέρετο, οὐ μὴν δὲ ἀλλὰ καὶ τὰ τῶν γονάτων αὐτῷ νεῦρα συγκόψαντες, ἡμιθανῆ καταλείπουσι. Τοῦτον οἱδοιπόροι τινὲς οὕτω κείμενον θεασάμενοι καὶ φιλανθρώπως διαβαστάσαντες εἰς τὴν πόλιν "Αγκυραν ἀποφέρουσιν, ἐκ γειτόνων οὖσαν, καὶ τῷ


[1030] {#pg.116.1030}
etiam nomen filii, Zenonem, dicens, o mulier, vocabis filium. Et sic quidem sanctus. Ego autem nihil dicam amplius. Clarum est enim ita evenisse; nihil enim excidebat ex iis quæ ab illo prædicebantur. Propter sanctum quoque timens imperator, et ei omnino repugnare declinans, eum aggreditur arte et blanditiis, et cum ipse sæpe eum, et per multos, ut ad ipsum veniret precatus, non persuadebat, ipse ad eum venit, et ritu supplicis adorat, et illius pedes prehensat, rogans ut sibi detur venia. Ille autem (quis enim ad ea videnda erat ipso perspicacior ?) illum quidem multis tunc probris insequitur. Deinde etiam aperit id quod multos latebat : Humilitas illa, dicens, et contritio est artificiosa simulatio, densa pelle tegens importunitatem ejus et immanitatem, sed cito videbitis, qui omnia piscit Dei oculum, et validam manum, quæ etiam potentes dejicit. Atque cum sic quidem sanctus tam multa fecisset miracula, Deique vertisset et profligasset hostem, et res Ecclesiæ bene constituisset, quin etiam futura prædixisset, revertitur ad columnam, et certamina ac labores magno et forti animo suscipit.
XLIX. Non multum interfluxit temporis, et Basiliscus quidem ut a magno prædictum fuerat Daniele, a regia expellitur, et Zeno rursus sceptra tenet, et ei acceperunt ejus prædictiones, et ai magnum Danielem veno Zeno cum uxore, videns quidem convenientes exitus prædictionum, eventu autem dignas redditurus gratias. Jam vero finem quærente oratione, et cum magni Danielis vita volente simul requiescere, in illius miraculis adhuc parum perjucunde immorabimur. Vir quidam artificio aurifex habebat puerum, qui septimum quidem jam annum attingebat, nondum autem poterat ingredi, sed serpentium ritu ventre utens, se movendo reptabat. Sumpserunt eum parentes, et posuerunt ante columnam; ipsi autem infra miserabiliter et flebiliter ejulantes, ipsos quoque qui videbant, provocabant ad lacrymas. Sanctus vero ex alto despiciens, cum partim quidem increpando, partim autem admonendo eorum luctum sedasset, jubet ut perseverent in sacra æde Simeonis, et venerandis utantur reliquiis, et ea tanquam aliquod solvens medicamentum, morbo vinctis imponant pedibus. Dixit, et facta sunt. Et septimo die cum de more precatio fieret a beato (o admirabilium operum opifex Domine !), vident statim puerum salientem, et gradus ascendentem, et columnam amplectentem.
L. Quidam alius iter faciens ab Oriente, incidit in latrones, qui cum ei plagas, ut cum sacra dicam Scriptura, imposuissent, quæ secum habebat, auferunt; quin etiam cum ejus genuum nervos exsecuissent, relinquunt semimortuum. Eum quidam viatores cum vidissent sic jacentem, et benigne sustulissent, ferunt in propinquam civitatem Ancyram, et id civitatis episcopo significant, a quo mul-


[1031] {#pg.116.1031}
torum medicorum traditus manibus, cum ad eum  curandum ab eis adhibitum esset omne studium, sanantur quidem vulnera, sed nequaquam restituitur ad hoc, ut posset ingredi. Nondum enim is revera sacram anchoram apprehenderat Danielem. Cum multis ergo lacrymis persuasisset episcopo, ut ipsum ad magnum mitteret Danielem (hac enim de causa jurabat se iter esse ingressum), tanquam onus inane impositum jumentis, ducitur ad sanctum. Deinde columnae impositus, quid non ad movendam dicebat misericordiam? quas non vel maximas afferebat lamentationes? Clamabat, lacrymabatur, manibus petebat, oculis revocabat in memoriam iter propter eum susceptum, ostendebat mala quae acceperat a latronibus. Sed hic mihi considera, quam esset hic magnus a fastu alienus, et  quantum oderat gloriam; et quemadmodum in fugiendo honore tantum posuerat studii, quantum alii ad gloriam consequendam feruntur cupiditate. Ut enim alii ascriberent curationem, eos transmittebat ad Simeonis oratorium; etiamsi major ad ipsum ex eo rediret gloria. Sed revertendum est, unde digressi sumus. Postquam enim ille portatus fuit ad templum Simeonis, ad quod jussus fuerat, et quod ex reliquiis sumitur, oleo quotidie ungebatur, jam quidem erat sextus dies circa auroram, cum staret magnus Daniel ad cantica matutina, idem huic quoque accidit, quod priori; nam et repente consistit supra pedes, et cursim gradus  ascendit, et columnam amplexus, Deo et ejus ministro agebat gratias. Sed cur non dico id, quod est majus? Quidam Hippasius, et auctoritate et fide centurio erat, sicut is qui celebratur in Evangeliis. Hic ergo si quando vidisset aliquem ex familiaribus, aut cognatis, aut filiis, aut servis morbo implicatum, a sancto scriptis verbum solum requirebat, et aegrotanti imponens epistolam, statim morbum tanquam aliquem fugitivum fugabat. Cum unius adhuc meminero, accedam ad magni Danielis migrationem.
LI. Mulier quaedam habens filium, duodecim annos natum, ab ortu mutum; ea, inquam, cum latenter accessisset, et in sancti mandra charissimum posuisset, cum bona spe recedit. Vident itaque monachi puerum, et cum eum sumpsissent, ducunt ad sanctum. Ille vero cum eum solum vidisset, jussit ut cum eis versaretur, futurus ipse Dei minister. Postquam autem significarunt beato puerum esse mutum, jubet illius linguam ungi oleo sanctorum. Quis praeterierit ea quae sunt postea consecuta? Suspicantur matrem propter paupertatem mentiendo dixisse filium esse mutum, ut ex eo aleretur filius: et accedentes, alii quidem ei incutiebant terrorem, ut sic conturbatus loqueretur; alii autem telis pungebant dormientem, aut linguis acutis, et repente e somnis excitabant, ut clamasse, et aliquid interim dixisse deprehenderetur. Is vero cum vellet ipse quidem multa dicere prae dolore, valido autem morbi vinculo linguam haberet prae-


[1032] {#pg.116.1032}
ἐπισκόπῳ τῆς πόλεως δῆλον ποιοῦσι, παρ᾿ οὗ πολλῶν ἰατρῶν χερσὶν ἐκδοθεὶς καὶ πολλῆς τῆς σπουδῆς παρ᾿ ἐκείνων ἀξιωθεὶς τὰ τραύματα μὲν ὑγιάζεται, κύριος δὲ τοῦ βαδίζειν οὐδαμῶς γίνεται. Οὔπω γὰρ τῆς ἱερᾶς ἀγκύρας ὁ Δανιὴλ αὕτη κατὰ τὸ ἀληθὲς, δραξάμενος ἦν. Πολλοῖς οὖν δάκρυσι τὸν ἐπίσκοπον πείσας ἐπὶ τὸν μέγαν αὐτὸν ἀποστεῖλαι, τούτου γὰρ ἕνεκα καὶ διώμνυτο τὴν πορείαν ποιήσασθαι, οἷα φόρτος ἄψυχος τοῖς ὑποζυγίοις ἐπιτεθείς, ἀπάγεται πρὸς τὸν ὅσιον. Εἶτα τῷ στύλῳ παρατεθεὶς, τί μὴ πρὸς οἶκτον ἔλεγε, τί μὴ πρὸς ἔλεον ἐποίει, ποίαν θρήνων ὑπερβολὴν ἀπελίμπανεν; Ἐβόα, ἐδάκρυε, ταῖς χερσὶν ᾐτεῖτο, τοῖς ὀφθαλμοῖς τῆς δι᾿ αὐτὸν ὁδοῦ ὑπεμνήσκετο, τὰ ἐκ τῶν λῃστῶν ἐδείκνυ κακά. Ἀλλ᾿ ἐνταῦθά μοι σκόπει τοῦ δικαίου τὸ ἄτιμον καὶ μισόδοξον · καὶ ὅπως τοσαύτην εἶχε πρόνοιαν τοῦ φεύγειν τιμὴν ὅση τοῖς ἄλλοις τοῦ δόξης ἀπολαύειν ἐπιθυμία. Ἵνα γὰρ ἑτέρῳ τὴν ἴασιν ἐπιγράψωνται, τῷ τοῦ Συμεὼν αὐτοῦς εὐκτηρίῳ παρέπεμπεν, εἰ καὶ οὕτω μάλιστα μεῖζον αὐτῷ ἡ δόξα περιηχεῖτο. Ἀλλ᾿ ἐπανιτέον ὅθεν ἐξέβημεν. Ὡς γὰρ ἐκεῖνος τοῖς βαστάσασσιν εἰς ὃν προσέτετακτο τοῦ Συμεὼν ἀπηνέχθη νεὼν καὶ τῷ ἐκ τῶν λειψάνων ἐλαίῳ καθικάστην ἐχρίετο, ἕκτη μὲν ἦν ἄρτι περὶ τὴν ἕω, τοῦ μεγάλου περὶ τὰς ἑωθινὰς ᾠδὰς ἱσταμένου ταυτὸν τῷ προτέρῳ καὶ περὶ τοῦτον ἐγίνετο. Ἄφνω τε γὰρ ἐπὶ τοὺς πόδας ἵσταται, καὶ δρομαίως τοὺς βαθμοὺς ἀναβαίνει, καὶ τῷ στύλῳ περιπλακεὶς τῷ Θεῷ καὶ τῷ τούτου θεράποντι τὴν εὐχαριστίαν ἐδίδου. Ἀλλὰ τί μὴ μεῖζον λέγω; Ἱππάσιός τις τὴν ἀξίαν καὶ τὴν πίστιν ἑκατοντάρχης, ὥσπερ πρώην ὁ ἐν τοῖς Εὐαγγελίοις περιιαδόμενος · οὗτος τοίνυν, εἴποτέ τινα τῶν οἰκείων ἢ συγγενῶν ἢ παίδων ἢ δούλων νόσῳ ἑώρα περισεχθέντα, ῥῆμα μόνον διὰ γραφῆς ἐπεζήτει τὸν ὅσιον, καὶ τὴν ἐπιστολὴν τῷ νοσοῦντι ἐπιτιθεὶς εὐθέως ὥσπερ τινὰ δραπέτην τὴν νόσον φεύγειν ἐποίει. Ἑνὸς ἔτι μνησθεὶς ἐπὶ τὴν τοῦ μεγάλου μετάστασιν βαδιδοῦμαι.
ΝΑ΄. Γυνή τις υἱὸν ἔχουσα δύο πρὸς τοῖς δέκα χρόνοις τὴν ἡλικίαν ἐκ γενετῆς ἄλαλον, αὕτη κατὰ τὸ λεληθὸς προσελθοῦσα καὶ τῇ μάνδρᾳ τοῦ ὁσίου προθεῖσα τὸν φίλτατον μετὰ χρηστῶν ἄπεισι τῶν ἐλπίδων. Ὁρῶσι τοίνυν οἱ μοναχοὶ τὸν παῖδα, καὶ λαβόντες ἄγουσι πρὸς τὸν ὅσιον. Ὁ δὲ καὶ μόνον ἰδὼν ἐκέλευε συνεῖναι τούτοις λειτουργὸν αὐτὸν τοῦ Θεοῦ ἐσόμενον. Ἐπειδὴ δὲ καὶ τὸ ἄλαλον εἶναι τὸν παῖδα τῷ μακαρίῳ ἐγνώρισαν, χρίεσθαι τῷ τῶν ἁγίων ἐλαίῳ τὴν ἐκείνου γλῶτταν προστάσσει. Τίς ἂν παραδράμοι τὰ μετὰ ταῦτα; Ὑποπτεύουσι τὴν μητέρα διὰ πενίαν ἐπιψεύσασθαι τῷ φιλτάτῳ τὴν ἀφωνίαν, ἵν᾿ αὐτόθεν ὁ παῖς αὐτῇ διατρέφοιτο · καὶ προσιόντες οἱ μὲν αὐτῷ φίδους ἴστων καὶ δείλματα, ἵνα ἐκταραχθεὶς φθέγξηται, οἳ δὲ καθεύδοντα βέλεσιν ἔπλησσον ἢ ξύλοις ὀξέσι, καὶ αἰφνιδίως τῶν ὕπνων ἀνίστων, ἵνα καὶ βοήσας; ἢ τι παρειπὼν φωραθῇ. Ὁ δὲ πολλὰ μὲν καὶ αὐτὸς ὑπὸ τῆς ὀδύνης ἐθέλων εἰπεῖν, τῷ δὲ τοῦ πάθους ἰσχυρῷ δεσμῷ τὴν γλῶτταν πεπεδημένος


[1033] {#pg.116.1033}
ἄφωνος ἦν, πολλὰ βοῶν τῷ τῆς καρδίας πρὸς τὸν Θεὸν στόματι. Ἦκεν ἡ πρώτη κυρία τῶν ἡμερῶν, καὶ τοῦ διακόνου τὸ τοῦ Χριστοῦ Εὐαγγέλιον συνήθως μέλλοντος ὑπαναγινώσκειν, ὡς πρότερον, τὸ εὐαγγελιστοῦ ὄνομα τοῖς λαοῖς ἐξεφώνησε, προφθάσας πάντας ὁ παῖς, τὸ, Δόξα σοι, Κύριε, καθαρᾷ, ἔφη, καὶ μεγίστῃ φωνῇ· καὶ τὸ λοιπὸν οὕτω τῆς ἱερᾶς λειτουργίας συμφάλλων τῷ πλήθει καὶ συναποκρινόμενος διετέλεσε. Πολλὰ μὲν οὖν καὶ ἄλλα τῷ μεγάλῳ κατώρθωται· μήτε λόγῳ ῥητά, μήτε διὰ τὸν κόρον ἀκοῇ χωρητά. ΝΒ΄. Πλὴν ἀρκέσει καὶ ταῦτα δεῖξαι· ὅσην ἐκεῖνος ἐπλούτει τὴν πρὸς Θεὸν παρρησίαν, καὶ ὅσην κἂντεῦθεν εἶχε τὴν πρὸς ἐκεῖνον ἐγγύτητα. Τοῦτο δὲ μόνον ἐρῶ καὶ ὥσπερ εἰ τὰ ῥηθέντα ἐπισφραγίσομαι, ὅτι· τοσοῦτον ἦν αὐτῷ μετὰ πολλῶν καὶ μεγίστων θαυμάτων τὸ ἐπιεικές τε καὶ μέτριον, καὶ οὐχ ἁπλῶς μέτριον, ἀλλὰ καὶ συντετριμμένον, κατὰ τὸν θεῖον φάναι Δαυΐδ, καὶ εἰς ἄκρον ἧκον τῆς ταπεινώσεως, ὥστε οὐ μᾶλλον ἦν ἐθαύμασεν αὐτὸν τῆς ἐνεργείας τῶν παραδόξων ὅσον τοῦ τοιαῦτα διενεργοῦντα μηδὲν πλέον ἔχειν τῶν πολλῶν οἴεσθαι. Ἀμέλει· καὶ τοσοῦτον εἰς ἀρετὴν προσέχων σφόδρα περὶ τοῦ κρίνειν τὰ τῶν ἄλλων εὐλαβῶς εἶχε καὶ πεπεισμένος. Καὶ τὸ ἐν παντὶ δὲ περὶ Θεοῦ καὶ τῶν ὑπὲρ ἡμᾶς τι διδάσκειν οὕτως ἐπίφοβον ἦν αὐτῷ καὶ πεφυλαγμένον, ὡς κατέ ποτέ τινων περὶ θεολογίας φιλοπευστοῦντων, καὶ ἄτοπά τινα δῆθεν ὑπὸ ἱερέων τολμώμενα προτεινόντων καὶ διενοχλούντων αὐτῷ, εἰ μὲν περὶ Θεοῦ, φησίν, ἐστὶν ὁ ἀγών, αὐτάρκης ἡμῖν ὁ τῶν ἀποστόλων χορὸς καὶ αἱ θεολόγοι τῶν Πατέρων φωναί, καὶ οὐδὲν δέον περαιτέρω περιεργάζεσθαι· εἰ δὲ καὶ περὶ ἀνθρωπίνων πραγμάτων καὶ ἱερέων ὑμῖν τὸ ἀμφίβολον, ταῦτα πάντα τῇ τοῦ Θεοῦ κρίσει συγχωρητέον καὶ τοῖς ἐξ ἐκείνου τὴν τοιαύτην παραλαβοῦσιν ἀρχήν. Ἡμῖν δὲ ἀγαπητὸν καὶ ὑφ᾿ ἑτέρων ποιμαίνεσθαι καὶ τὰ καθ᾿ ἡμᾶς σκοπεῖν, ἀλλὰ μὴ τῶν περιττῶν τούτων καὶ βλαβερῶν ἔχεσθαι ζητημάτων. Καὶ ταῦτα μὲν οὕτως.
ΝΓ΄. Ὁ δὲ λόγος ἤδη πρὸς τὸ τέλος ἥκοντος τοῦ ἁγίου, καὶ αὐτὸς μέλλει καταλήγειν εἰς σιωπήν. Πλὴν ἀλλὰ μικρά τινα κἀνταῦθα πρὸ τοῦ τέλους εἰπόντες οὕτω τὸν λόγον διαναπαύσομαι. Ἔγνω μὲν ὁ προφητικὸς ἐκεῖνος σὺν ἅπασι τοῖς λοιποῖς καὶ τὴν ἑαυτοῦ πρὸς Κύριον ἐκδημίαν καὶ τοῖς μαθηταῖς τοῦτο φανερῶς προηγόρευσεν. Ἐπεὶ δὲ ἡ κυρία ἤδη ἐγγίζουσα ἦν ἐγγράφως αὐτοῖς τὰ τῆς εὐλογίας ὥσπερ τινὲς ἄλλοι τὰ τῆς οὐσίας χαρίζονται, καὶ ὑπογραφεὺς αὐτῷ διηκόνει, καὶ οὕτως ἔχουσα ἡ διάταξις· Ἐγώ, φησί, τέκνα καὶ ἀδελφοί (ἀμφότερα γὰρ ὑμεῖς), τὸ μὲν ὅτι πνευματικῶς ὑμᾶς ὠδῖνα, τὸ δὲ ὅτι κοινῶς ἁπάντων ὁ Θεός· πρὸς τοῦτον ἤδη τὸν κοινὸν Πατέρα πορεύομαι. Πλὴν οὐκ ὀρφανοὺς ἐάσω τοὺς ἐμοὶ ποθεινούς, πατρὸς ἐρημίαν ὀδυρομένους, ἀλλ᾿ ὑπ᾿ ἐκείνῳ πατρὶ τὴν ὑμῶν πρόνοιαν καταλείπω, ὃς δὴ τὰ πάντα ὑμῶν ἐμὲ πεποίηκεν. Αὐτὸς οὖν ὁ


[1034] {#pg.116.1034}
peditam, erat mutus, ore cordis multa loquens ad Deum. Venit dierum primus et præcipuus; et cum diaconus Christi Evangelium esset de more lecturus, cum primum nomen evangelistæ effatus est populis, omnes puer præveniens, dixit pura et maxima voce, ‹ Gloria tibi, Domine; › et sic deinceps per totum sacrum ministerium perseveravit simul cum populo canere et respondere. Atque multa quidem alia a magno Daniele gesta sunt, quæ neque verbis explicari possunt, neque propter satietatem capi auribus. LII. Verum enim vero sufficient hæc ad ostendendum, quanta ille fiducia esset dives apud Deum, et quanta ei cum illo intercederet conjunctio. Hoc autem solum dicam, et ea quæ dicta sunt, veluti obsignabo, quod cum multis et maximis miraculis, tanta erat ei lenitas et moderatio; et non solum moderatio, sed ut cum divino David dicam ¹⁹, contritio et summa humilitas, ut non magis eum quis admiretur propter operationem miraculorum, quam quod cum talia operaretur, nihil existimaret se plus habere aliis. Quin etiam cum virtute tantum præstaret, in judicio de aliis ferendo erat admodum cautus et parcus. Quam vero esset in omni re a fastu et superbia alienus, quis dixerit? Eorum autem, quæ supra nos sunt, adeo timebat et cavebat docere aliquid, ut cum quidam, qui de theologia libenter sciscitabantur, quædam etiam absurda proponerent, quæ audebant facere sacerdotes, et ei exhiberent molestiam : Si a vobis quidem, diceret de Deo disceptatio, vobis sufficit chorus apostolorum, et quæ de Deo disserunt Patrum voces, neque opus est curiose ulterius inquirere. Quod si de rebus humanis et sacerdotibus vobis est controversia, sunt hæc omnia Dei judicio relinquenda, et iis qui ab illo talem acceperunt administrationem : nobis vero satis esse debet, si ab aliis pascamur, et ea consideremus et inquiramus, quæ ad nos pertinent, non autem has supervacaneas et noxias persequamur quæstiones. Et hæc quidem sic habent.
LIII. Oratio autem, cum jam veniat sanctus ad finem, desitura est ipsa quoque in silentium. Verum enimvero cum hic quoque pauca quædam ante finem dixerimus, sic sistemus orationem. Cognovit quidem vir ille propheticus cum reliquis omnibus, suam quoque ad Dominum migrationem, idque aperte suis prædixit discipulis. Cum autem præstitutus dies jam appropinquaret, benedictionem eis reliquit in scriptis, sicut quidam alii facultates, eique deserviit scriba, et tale fuit ejus testamentum : Ego, inquit, o filii et fratres (ambo enim vos estis, tum quod vos genui spiritualiter, tum vero, quod Deus est communis pater omnium), ad hunc communem Patrem jam vado, sed non orbos relinquam mihi dilectos, deflentes patris solitudinem, sed illi Patri vestri relinquo curam, qui cum vobis omnibus me creavit. Ipse ergo, qui omnia fabrica-


[1035] {#pg.116.1035}
tus est ratione et sapientia, et deinde cœlos quoque inclinavit, et descendit in terram¹¹, et mortuus est, et resurrexit pro nobis, ipse erit vobiscum : ut sapiens quidem, vos servans a maligno; ut Dominus autem, ad suam conservans voluntatem; ut pater vero, si forte aberretis, benigne revocans, et spiritualiter ulnas nobis extendens; ut autem ut se pro nobis ad mortem tradiderit, vos etiam inter vos invicem conjunget concordia, et efficiet, ut sitis unum spiritu. Sectemini humilitatem, servite obedientia, amate hospitalitatem, jejunium, vigiliam, paupertatem, et quod est primum et maximum præceptum, charitatem; quæ ad pietatem pertinent, recte tenete. Hæreticorum zizania cavete; a matre vestra Ecclesia minime separemini. Si hæc omnia feceritis, eritis virtute perfecti.
LIV. Hæc piis labris dictavit, et jussit legi ab omnibus monachis, stantibus ad scalarum gradus, et deflentibus illius separationem. Dicitur autem hoc visum esse circa eum; nempe tribus diebus ante mortem, jam media nocte concurrisse et ad eum convenisse sanctos, qui fuerunt a sæculo, prophetas, apostolos, martyres, et omnes denique sanctos, qui et magnum benigne salutarunt, et jusserunt eum divinum celebrare mysterium. Quod quidem celebravit, et intemerata ipsa sumpsit sacramenta, et iis quibus par erat impertit. Atque jam quidem magnus extremum agebat spiritum, et ipse antistes episcoporum (is erat Euphemius), jam enim mortuus erat Acacius, cum aliis veniebat ad emigrationem. Aderat autem Rais quoque fidelissima, et vir quidam, qui possidebatur a dæmone, pergebat ad columnam; qui et sanctorum ad sanctum adventum clamando aperte significabat, et eos suis appellabat nominibus, et ipsorum, ut qui convenissent, meminerat angelorum. Deinde etiam adjiciebat fore ut tertia hora diei ipse Daniel excedat ad Dominum, et a diuturna habitatione immundus expellatur spiritus. Quæ utraque suo tempore evenerunt. Quæ prius dicta est autem Rais, magnifice parabat ea quæ pertinebant ad sepulturam; et cum multos adduxisset artifices, et jussisset fieri tumulum in inferiore parte basis columnæ, et usque ad fastigium, statuit ex utraque parte tumuli homines, qui faces ferrent, et lampades tenerent manibus, et ea canerent, quæ cani solent in decessu. Cumque illa corpus infra detulisset, in plumbeo id ponit loculo, in hoc quoque servans magni Danielis testamentum. Ille enim mandavit discipulis, seu imperatores, seu reges, aut ulli alii, se invicem superare contendant in loculi sumptu et magnificentia, nolite suscipere : sed illic et sic corpus deponi sinite, ubi et quomodo voluerit Rais fidelissima. Hæc ergo peracta sunt, et expulsus est immundus dæmon, cum multum discerpsisset.
LV. Porro autem quædam quoque alia effecta sunt


[1036] {#pg.116.1036}
πάντα λόγῳ καὶ σοφίᾳ δημιουργήσας, εἶτα καὶ οὐρανοὺς κλίνας, καὶ κατελθὼν ἐπὶ γῆς, καὶ ὑπὲρ ἡμῶν θανὼν τε καὶ ἀναστὰς, αὐτὸς ἔσται μεθ' ὑμῶν· ὡς μὲν σοφός, φυλάττων ἀπὸ τοῦ πονηροῦ, ὡς δὲ δεσπότης εἰς τὸ αὐτοῦ θέλημα συντηρῶν, ὡς δὲ καὶ πατὴρ, κἂν εἴ τι σφαλῆτε, φιλανθρώπως ἀνακαλούμενος καὶ πνευματικῶς ὑμῖν τὰς ἀγκάλας ἁπλῶν· ὡς δὲ καὶ ὑπὲρ ἡμῶν εἰς θάνατον προέμενος ἑαυτὸν, καὶ ὑμᾶς ἀλλήλοις ὁμονοίᾳ συνάψει, καὶ ἓν εἶναι παρασκευάσει τῷ πνεύματι. Ταπεινοφροσύνης ἔχεσθε, ὑπακοῇ δουλεύετε, φιλοξενίας ἐρᾶτε, νηστείας, ἀγρυπνίας, ἀκτημοσύνης, τῆς πρώτης καὶ μεγίστης τῶν ἐντολῶν ἀγάπης, τὰ πρὸς εὐσέβειαν ὀρθῶς ἔχοιτε, τὰ ζιζάνια τῶν αἱρετικῶν φυλάττεσθε, τῆς μητρὸς ὑμῶν τῆς Ἐκκλησίας κομιδῇ μὴ χωρίζεσθε. Ἂν ταῦτα πάντα ποιῆτε, τέλειοι τὴν ἀρετὴν ἔσεσθε.
ΝΔ'. Ταῦτα φιλοθέοις χείλεσιν ὑπηγόρευσε, καὶ ὑπαναγνωσθῆναι πᾶσι τοῖς μοναχοῖς ἐκέλευσε, περὶ τοὺς βαθμοὺς τῆς κλίμακος ἱσταμένοις, καὶ τὸν ἐκείνου χωρισμὸν ὀλοφυρομένοις. Λέγεται δὲ καὶ τοῦτο περὶ αὐτὸν θεαθῆναι τρισὶ πρότερον τῆς τελευτῆς ἡμέραις· νυκτὸς ἤδη μεσούσης συνδραμεῖν πάντας καὶ πρὸς αὐτὸν ἀπαντῆσαι τοὺς ἀπ' αἰῶνος ἁγίους, προφήτας, ἀποστόλους, μάρτυρας καὶ πάντας τοὺς ὁσίους, ἀλλὰ καὶ δυνάμεις τινὰς οὐρανίους, οἳ φιλοφρόνως τὸν μέγαν ἠσπάζοντο, καὶ τὴν θείαν ἐπιτελέσαι μυσταγωγίαν ἐπέτρεπον, ἣν καὶ ᾠφῃ τελέσας αὐτός τε τῶν ἀχράντων μυστηρίων μεταλαβὼν καὶ μεταδεδωκὼς οἷς ἦν ἄξιον. Ἄρτι μὲν οὖν ὁ μέγας περὶ τὰς ἐσχάτας ἀναπνοὰς ἦν, καὶ αὐτὸς ὁ τῶν ἱερέων πρόεδρος, Εὐφήμιος οὗτος ἦν (ἤδη γὰρ Ἀκάκιος ἐτεθνήκει), μετὰ τῶν εἰς τὴν ἐκδημίαν παρῆν, παρῆν δὲ καὶ ἡ πιστοτάτη Ῥαΐς, καί τις ἀνὴρ δαίμονι κάτοχος· πρὸς τὸν στῦλον ἐχώρει, ὃς τήν τε τῶν ἁγίων ἐπιδημίαν τὴν πρὸς τὸν ὅσιον φανερῶς ἐβόα, ὀνομαστί τε τούτους ἐκάλε, καὶ τῶν ἀγγέλων ὡς δραμόντων ἐμέμνητο, εἶτα καὶ προσετίθει ὅτι τρίτην ὥραν τῆς ἡμέρας ἐχούσης αὐτός τε πρὸς Κύριον ὁ Δανιὴλ ἐπελεύσεται, καὶ τὸ ἀκάθαρτον πνεῦμα τῆς χρονίας οἰκήσεως θεοκινήτως ἀπελαθήσεται, ἃ δὴ ἀμφότερα κατὰ τὸν καιρὸν ἀπήντα. Ἡ μέντοι προῤῥηθεῖσα Ῥαΐς φιλοτίμως τὰ πρὸς ταφὴν ἐνήργει, καὶ συχνοὺς τῶν τεχνιτῶν ἀγαγοῦσα στύρακά τε γενέσθαι προσέταξε κάτωθεν τοῦ στύλου βάσεως καὶ μέχρι τῆς κορυφῆς δᾳδούχους ἓξ ἑκατέρου μέρους τοῦ στύρακος περιέστησε, ταῖς χερσὶ λαμπάδας κατέχοντας καὶ ᾄδοντας τὰ ἐξόδια, καὶ οὕτω τὸν νεκρὸν ἐκείνη κατενεγκοῦσα ἐν σορῷ τίθησι μολυβδίνῃ, σῴζουσα κἂν τούτῳ τὴν τοῦ μεγάλου διάταξιν. Ἐπέσκηψε γὰρ ἐκεῖνος τοῖς μαθηταῖς ὅτι κἂν βασιλεῖς κἂν ἄλλοι τινὲς εἶεν πολιτείᾳ περὶ τὴν σορὸν ὑπερβαλεῖν ἀλλήλους φιλονεικοῦντες μὴ παραδέξασθαι, ἀλλ' ἐκεῖσε δὴ καὶ οὕτως τὸ σῶμα κατατεθῆναι, ἔνθα δηλαδὴ καὶ ὅπως ἡ πιστοτάτη Ῥαΐς βούλοιτο. Καὶ ὁ ἀκάθαρτος δαίμων πολλὰ σπαράξας τὸν ἄνθρωπον ἀπηλαύνετο.
ΝΕ'. Καὶ ἄλλα δέ τινα θαύματα κατεπράχθη. Τρεῖς
XXV. Since, however, the bishop and those who followed the bishop pleaded much for the ladder to be set in place, that he might ascend, the unconquered one forbade it. But the sun was already standing at high noon, striking their very heads with its hot rays, and the bishop saw that the multitude was being scorched by thirst and heat. So, what did he do? Below, he directed the archdeacon to recite the customary prayers, while he himself, having prayed, ordained the great man, even though he was not present, as a presbyter. At that moment the multitude shouted, "Worthy!" over him who was truly worthy. Thus, when the Patriarch Gennadius had named Daniel a priest and had said, "Behold, the people have sent forth for thee, according to custom, the voice that has come to pass regarding this, and God from above has laid His hand upon thee through me," and when he had said simply that he had received all the symbols of the priesthood, he added, "It remains, therefore, for the ladder to be leaned against the column, so that thou mayest also partake of the sacred sacrifice, and mayest yield in everything to God who calls thee." The blessed man, realizing that these things could not have happened without God, and that God Himself had given His consent to what had taken place, ordered the ladder to be set up, and he gave and received the heavenly pearl—I mean the divine communion. Then, having performed all the other things that are customary for priests to perform, and having prayed for the multitude, he sent them away, at once rejoicing and marveling; marveling, on the one hand, at his foreknowledge and his truly praiseworthy timidity, and on the other, that though with difficulty, he was nonetheless persuaded himself to receive the priesthood, and to provide them with the prayers and the blessing. These things were also made known to the Emperor, and they filled him with similar joy and astonishment.
XXVI. Since he had not taken on the great pride that is proper to imperial rule, but had learned how much virtue is more precious than empire, he himself also ascended to him. And just as if he had left below and cast off his royal vanity and haughty brow, he threw himself upon the ground and touched his holy feet, as if by doing this he were more honored than in being an emperor. When he had touched them, he was filled with astonishment, just as Gelasius had been before him; for those feet were extremely swollen and entirely covered with sores, and it was at once horrifying and piteous to see and to touch them. Then, therefore, the Emperor bestowed upon him a base of double columns, which he himself had set up, calling it a double column; and as Daniel stepped upon it, he rejoiced. For it was lifted higher in height, and thus it was extended for the purpose of his spiritual exercise—in summer, with the sun striking him more hotly, and in winter, with the air colder. And when the force of the winds, of snow, and of hail attacked him vigorously and demonstrated a constancy that was by no means endurable, no doubt many miracles followed. It is not easy for me, avoiding length of narrative, to tell of all of these, but only of the most essential ones, as I have promised. But that which I could not leave out even if I wished, this I will now endeavor to relate, as best I can in my discourse.
XXVII. The circuit of the year had already reached its beginning, and the month which the Romans call September was at hand, in which those who love God celebrate the feast of the great martyr Mamas. When evening came, therefore, and the pious were concluding the rites of the festival and keeping an all-night vigil, the prophecy of the great Daniel reached its end. Suddenly, in the greatest queen of cities, a conflagration began, and it was so fanned and prevailed to such a degree that it well-nigh consumed the whole city. It began, indeed, at the wall that is by the sea, which they also call the *neorion* [dockyards], and it reached as far as the Forum of Constantine and extended to the harbor of Julian, as if it had girdled the middle of the city. In that place all bodies of people were engulfed; and whatever was caught was easily destroyed, and the fire, being thus fed with timber, rose into a most mighty flame. It was an unconquerable evil, for it was proved that whatever was brought to offer aid achieved nothing. Because of this, it devoured many houses and many temples, and a multitude of men and women as it spread. Many were immediately mutilated in the parts and members of their bodies which the fire touched, and they were half-burnt and half-cut away, becoming more pitiable to their own kin than those who had utterly perished with all they had. There was, therefore, a danger that the miracle of the world, as it is commonly called—Constantine’s city, I mean—might be seen to suffer things similar to the burning of Sodom. With difficulty, at last, in such a great evil, the prediction of the holy Daniel came to their minds, and they believed that the evil would be conquered by his prayers alone. Hence it came about that they ran to him with great speed; one lamenting the loss of money, another of servants, another of a house, another of friends, and one of kinsfolk, another of his wife and children, and yet another of all his possessions at once. By these things they caused the saint such grief that, filled with tears, he judged them for their negligence, since, though he had much testified and prophesied to them, they had not seen fit to avoid the evil through repentance; they were Ninevites only in suffering, not also in repenting. Saying these things, he exhorted them to take care for prayer and fasting; and he himself, lifting his hands to heaven, called upon the help from there, and after the prayer, when he had told them the end of the calamity, he dismissed them. "In seven days," he said, "the threats of the fire will cease." Thus he spoke, and the deed followed, and the fire was no more. Then fear seized the others, and all those in authority, and the emperor himself; and having taken his empress [with him], he became a piteous petitioner to the blessed Daniel, asking for mercy concerning what had passed, and for safety in the future. XXVIII. By now, after the autumnal equinox, the weather had changed and it was winter—a winter greater than speech can explain, yet it was overcome by the unconquered spirit of the noble man. Very many rains fell, and there was a vehement storm of winds. The winds themselves also stirred up a sedition against one another, and many blew at once, and so wild were they that even the iron grips of the columns, which
connected and joined the two columns together and supported Daniel on high, were violently torn away; he, however, was held fast by only a small column that happened to have been inserted between them. The storm, therefore, fell upon them at once, and the columns were shaken, and the righteous man on high was carried hither and thither like a branch of a tree and was swung about by the blast. Below, the disciples cried out with tears, fixing their eyes on him alone, and their one bitter expectation was the deprivation of their father. But the hope that Daniel had in the Lord did not waver; rather, fixed upon this unshakeable rock, the blessed man, speaking nothing to anyone, and having no fear even if this should happen (for what is death burdensome to him, for whom remaining here was, according to David, a prison?), kept his mind raised upward and continued in prayer. The righteous man cried out, therefore, and the Lord heard him immediately, and from above, from heaven, as once before from the ship, he rebuked the winds, and instantly there was a great calm and stillness. And so much for the winds.
XXIX. But since it was necessary that not only the winds, but also certain of those who were suffering from the madness of Arius should be restrained from their confusion, this too was provided through him most wisely, as is customary. And observe how Providence knows how to weave together various and manifold dispensations, and how it often connects one thing through another. The emperor knew what had happened to the holy man and made haste to see the column, partly to impose penalties on those who had not made the grips secure and sturdy, and partly to fasten them again for safety in the future. But the great meekness of Daniel rescued those men from the threats of the emperor, who was threatening them with death. Yet on his descent from the mountain, no small danger befell the emperor. For fear had by chance agitated the horse he was riding, and it, being terrified and having raised its front feet upwards, fell immediately supine upon the ground; the horse fell supine, and the emperor with it. The part of the imperial chair that stood raised in front struck the emperor’s forehead and knocked off the crown upon his head from a distance; at that moment, it happened that the pearls that were in it were scattered, one here and another there. But Daniel, having forestalled the evil one with prayer, saved the emperor from an evil death, and he performed his return without such danger. But here is the essence of my narrative, and how Providence knows how to manage things in a paradoxical way.
XXX. The emperor’s groom—or, as the common and familiar language might say, the count of the horses, named Jordan—had for all the previous time been suffering under the lawless madness of Arius. But then, fearing the emperor’s danger and also that the incident might become a cause of anger against him, he ran to the holy man. He, however, promised to deliver him not only from the fear of the leader [the emperor], but also to rid him of that wicked false opinion, which is more grievous than any other danger. The groom heard this, and with his meek and gentle persuasions, he abandoned the opinion of Arius
and now thinks as the orthodox do. The holy man therefore sent word to the emperor that Jordanes, having renounced his deception, now follows the correct doctrines; and that he is most righteous, and for this reason especially—which is also why he came here to me—that he might obtain forgiveness from you. The emperor wrote back, saying: "The cause of my danger was none other than myself, having dared to mount a horse before your eyes, instead of remaining on foot, not far from the holy column. From this time forward, I shall take careful heed of this. As for Jordanes, I am not only not angry with him, but I am even very glad that my falling from the horse was the cause for him, who was lying low, to rise up." Henceforth, the emperor’s reverence for the holy man was so great that he not only honored him himself as was fitting, but also showed him to others as if he were a heavenly being.
XXXI. Now not long after, when it happened that the king of the Lazi came to the Roman emperor for the sake of making a treaty, the emperor, leaving aside other things, took him and led him to the holy man. "This," he said, "is the miracle of my empire." And the barbarian, seeing his endurance and fortitude, was so struck with wonder that he worshiped with tears not only the saint, but also the column upon which he was set. He uttered many words from a mind that was well-instructed and had learned much, saying: "I thank you, O heavenly King, that to me, who had come to an earthly king, you have shown the heavenly manner of life of this man, and have deemed me worthy to be a spectator of such mysteries." Thus, the great man was considered so worthy of admiration by both of them that they both made him the arbiter for defining their treaties. Furthermore, when Gubazius, king of the Lazi, had returned home and recounted the miracle to the peoples subject to him, he always kept the saint in remembrance. He never sent to Byzantium without also sending letters to him, which were not limited to mere greetings, but were largely concerned with remembering him and praying for his kingdom. So much did all wonder at the just man’s endurance—kinsmen and guests, private citizens and kings, Romans and barbarians alike—that those approaching drew near to him as if to an angel, and those departing called for his help from afar, requesting something through his prayers, and they obtained it.
XXXII. What has been said above is indeed sufficient to make his endurance known, and [to show] that his struggles and trials were beyond that of a human. But since it is my earnest desire to omit nothing of importance, I shall add to what has been said something greater, which pertains to his fortitude and endurance and to the wonderment of Him who gave him strength. It was winter, and a glacial and very harsh winter at that; the tempests of the winds were again violent and more severe than those that had preceded them; and along with the wind, thick snow was falling. The winds, therefore, attacking him more vehemently, blew away the skin-cloak from his shoulders and flung it into a certain ravine below; and along with the winds, the
much and cold snow also beat against his face. But he (for he had no roof beneath which he could take refuge) all night long (O soul unconquered by the bonds of the body!) endured everything with his naked body—the bitterness of the season, the snow, the winds, the harsh congealing and freezing about the dawn. Day appeared, and the evil did not cease, but grew stronger, and the winter intensified. The disciples, moreover, had no means to peer out from the door, and there was no one to bring him aid. That whole day, then, and the following night, which was much more bitter, this noble man endured bravely and steadfastly.
Now it was already the third day of the storm, and the fierceness of the frost having abated, the more fervent of the disciples placed the ladder against it, and having ascended with great haste, they saw a horrifying sight, O Lord and God; and although what they expected to see was something even more fearful than that which they did behold, yet at the very appearance it seemed more terrible: the saint already stripped of his cloak, but encased on all sides by ice—his head, his face, and the other parts of his body—and not only this, but the body itself entirely frozen and, if one considers the cold and the solidification, turned into ice. One would have called him a man of ice (for what else?) or a corpse, because he was not even thought to have breath in him. Having, therefore, with difficulty melted the ice with warm water and also sprinkled his body and washed it with sponges, since after a good while he came to himself, and as if from death having again received his breath, he had recovered his voice, he said: “Why, I ask you, do you wish to cause me trouble, and vex me while I am sleeping sweetly? For as I was just now calling upon the Lord for aid, it happened that I fell into a light sleep. But well may it be with you, O children, who take such care of your father.” And, “Give me,” he said, “something with which to cover myself,” blushing, I think, on account of his nakedness, yet not feeling the need of warmth. So they brought him other garments and clothed him, having put on a robe of divine joy, as I may say with the prophet.
XXXIII. Much, then, after this, when the Christ-loving emperor was entreating and begging him (for these things had reached even him), saying: “If not for your own sake, yet for ours and for our benefit, spare these perilous and extreme rigors; and do not, by being almost the author of your own death, leave us orphans, and seem to be departing this life yourself before the time.” While the emperor was saying these things and more, and was entreating him, and at the same time clasping his feet, he reluctantly consented to have at least a small roof, out of reverence for the emperor's tears. And though I have said this many times, nevertheless I shall say it now as well: there was no greater miracle to the emperor than the endurance and life of the great Daniel. Therefore, all who were sent as envoys to him, whether they were kings or ambassadors, some indeed through others, and others even
by his own hand, he brought to the holy man. And they, in seeing rather than hearing, were struck with amazement, and thus they returned, being blessed and having become better in their souls. Now, that he had also received from above the gift of seeing future things as if they were already present has been shown in many ways in what has gone before; there is, however, a clear proof of this as well. A rumor was circulating everywhere that Gaiseric, king of the Vandals, was waging war against the Romans with great preparation and greater audacity, and that he had already arrived at Alexandria with a large force, wishing to make it a prize of war for himself. Consequently, there was no small struggle for those in office and for the emperor himself, who quickly ran to the customary remedy for his distress and made the rumor known to the holy man. But he, as if not foreseeing, but already seeing with his own eyes the things that were to happen, immediately said that Gaiseric would not only fail to capture Alexandria, but would also return home with nothing accomplished in all other respects as well. There is, in short, no need to add what came to pass for those who know the man; for it came about not long after, just as the God-bearing tongue of Daniel had foretold.
XXXIV. Because of these things and many others more marvelous than these, the emperor, wishing to reward the great Daniel, since he could not do so in any other way, asked to at least make a small house for his disciples: "So that they may have," he says, "a dwelling for their bodies." But the holy man said: "If you spoke of erecting a building, I cannot be pleased by it. For such a thing never belonged to the blessed Simeon. But if you wish to do me a favor that is to my liking, hasten to bring his relics here, and you will find that a most welcome gift for me." Then willingly the emperor orders that, on the one hand, a holy house be built for Simeon to the north of Daniel’s pillar; and, on the other hand, that houses also be erected for the guests who ascended the mountain and for the disciples of the great man, even though the saint forbade this repeatedly. When those who had been sent to Antioch had brought the relics, as the emperor had ordered, the great Daniel instructed the bishop of the city to proclaim and publicly announce the transfer of the tabernacle, and that he himself should sit in the emperor’s carriage, holding the relics in his bosom, and thus make the journey with psalms and hymns, and that when he had brought them to the temple of the commander of the host, Michael, he should celebrate the divine liturgy there. When these things were being carried out, and the relics were being escorted with a great and most magnificent retinue, the Lord was seen to perform many miracles through them along the way; to narrate these in detail is certainly not of the present time. But after those things pertaining to the relics reached their end, and the priest himself and all who accompanied him ascended with joy to the holy man, his most sweet soul poured out greatest thanks to them in his words, and truly caused much honey to flow into their souls, speaking to them of love for the poor and for brothers, of charity, of judgment and of recompense, and of other such things, and persuading them to weep for joy to such an extent that only those who have had experience of such tears can know.
XXXV. Nor did he show the excellence of his virtue only in the things he himself did, but also in the things suffered by those who were not ashamed to revile him. For instance, when one of those who were approaching him—a man who had erred concerning the faith—was once heaping reproaches upon the holy man, and those present and listening were rebuking him, the saint himself put his hand inside his garment, drew out a roasted fish, and said, "Behold, this man who in your judgment is abstinent, was eating this and many others before you arrived." He spoke, and immediately justice pursued the man. For after a short while, when he himself had truly eaten of the fish and had given some to his wife and children to eat, a madness seized them, and they were all seen to be possessed by a demon. Those who were present, therefore, perceiving the cause and pitying him for this double madness, took them and led them to the holy man. And he, who was not only gentle toward those who sinned, but even skilled in turning their calamities to the opposite, when he saw them confessing and begging, prayed and delivered them from the madness and from the more grievous demon of vainglory. And they, who after this had so greatly profited from his healing that they wished to have their thanksgiving recorded, fashioned his likeness in silver, placed themselves under his feet, and inscribed their names and the thanksgiving, and dedicated it in the very church of the Archangel. Such was the persuasion upon the lips of the blessed man, and so much was his discourse able—almost as much as his life—to charm the soul and persuade it to choose the narrow way, that he persuaded not only those who came up to him for this very purpose, or who were otherwise mild and gentle, to weep and to be romantically captured by his great love, but he also disposed the impudent and savage to the same things. And here is a clear proof. XXXVI. A certain man named Edranus, a Galatian by race, otherwise manly and valiant of hand, reared in blood and wars, and one who had nonetheless brought back many feats of arms, and had under him many men, all of them valiant in battle—the report of him came and reached the ears of the Emperor. He immediately summoned him and those under his command; he bestowed other honors upon the men, but to him he entrusted the care of his horses—it is customary to call him "comes," as the account has indicated above. He also sends him, as was his wont, to see the great Daniel and to receive his blessing. When he had gone to him, and had learned his life, and had been taught by his words the things that were truly profitable, and had been changed by a certain divine transformation, and had been turned from a wolf into a sheep, he bade a long farewell to campaigns and wars and the honors from the Emperor; and he who before that time had been exercised in nothing but blood and slaughter (for what else is a soldier, and a barbarian at that?), was suddenly a philosopher and a disciple of the gentle Christ, and he begged to shave his head and be enrolled among his followers. And since he also [besought] the great one with his entreat
entreaties to grant his request, he becomes a teacher of many others; and having called together all those who were with him—both kinsmen and acquaintances and those otherwise related to him—he urges them to follow the same path as he. His exhortation, indeed, was such that it could vie with the tongue of orators and carry off the victory; the most delightful parts of which I shall commit to memory, and I know that you, too, will shortly share in the joy of it. B 37. "I," he says, "have resolved for the future to serve the heavenly King; for I have persuaded myself that it will profit a man nothing if he gains the whole world, but suffers the loss of his own soul. And what profit comes to us from war, unless it be swords, and murders, and blood, and carnage? And what calamity does not accompany these, and, furthermore, the ruin of the soul, in which he who has fallen has fallen justly? For why should one wage war against those who are of the same race? And he who kills is altogether a parricide and cursed. For why, for the sake of money or empty glory, has he dared to undertake such an evil deed? But lions, O men, and leopards, and wolves, and if there be any beast more savage than these, never wage a common war among themselves; but to us, in whom there is by nature both gentleness and reason—which suppresses the ferocity that is contrary to nature, and indeed also stirs up pity and brings to memory our common nature—and above all, to whom the greatest of commandments is that we should neither commit an injury nor argue back at all if we are injured: and this while we have near at hand the example of that very One, our Lord, who, when He was being led to the slaughter, followed (and yet, how great is the difference between the Lord and His servants!)—how is it that these things are rightly ordered, when we have many rewards for not doing these things, and many punishments for doing them? But if it is necessary to retaliate against those who attack us, it is necessary by all means to call upon the great and high hand for our defense and vindication, so that, by acting in accordance with what He commands, we might inflict more severe blows upon our enemies. Therefore, if you wish, leave these things; but remain with me, and let us choose the life which is from God. But if not, let each one go his own way; I shall compel no one further, even in this following Christ, who, having honored us with many other things, has honored us above all with free will." C 38. Even if these words do not seem to belong to a barbarian, if someone considers the grace of the divine Spirit, and that he was worthy to attain it, he will certainly find that he was capable of saying these things, and things even more sublime than these. Shortly, however, the power of his persuasion was also shown. For he persuaded two barbarians, who had not even heard the divine doctrine by word of mouth, to abandon everything and live together with him; while to the others, having given them money from what he possessed and bid them farewell, he permitted them to go whither they would. He, however, and those with him, following the sacred rule of renunciation, receive the habit of monks from holy hands, and Edranus, having changed his name, is called Philotheos.
Nor did the emperor himself remain ignorant of what had taken place; but upon learning of the event, he bore it heavily, and sent men to reproach Edranus for his departure. When he found him to be immovable in his purpose—and this was also confirmed to him by letters from the holy man—he despaired of bringing him back and resolved to trouble him no further. As for Titus, he was so seized by the zeal and imitation of the great man that he was even curious about his nourishment, and thought it of great importance to learn what he ate. And since he did not see him after the evening chants when the other brothers retired to take their rest, he would secretly leave the dormitory and hide behind the columns to watch the holy man; so that, often failing to attend the nocturnal psalmody on this account, he provided the brothers with an impression of sloth. But when, after seven full days, he had learned nothing more, he revealed his thoughts to the holy man and asked him to tell him whether, if at all, he ate and drank. And when the latter assured him that he did, but added that he used only such nourishment as was sufficient to hold the vessel of the body together—for we do not live in order to indulge in luxuries, but rather we are nourished so that we may have life—he acknowledged his submission with an obeisance and departed; and from that point on, he practiced such restraint regarding food and sleep that he partook of food only so far as was necessary to sustain life.
XXXIX. But what manner of struggle did this noble man show against sleep? Having suspended himself in mid-air by ropes fastened to his armpits, he would extend a board against his chest, and place his book upon the board, so that he might read while suspended in this way; and if, overcome by exhaustion, he ever needed to turn to sleep, the board would receive his head, providing some small relief to the force of nature. Now, Titus was dear to the emperor himself on account of his virtue; and whenever the emperor went up to the holy man, he would also go to him and return, gaining great benefit from him. Having thus lived out his life in this manner, he fell asleep in peace in that same place. But not only did he look toward virtue in this way, but also one of his servants, a man named Anatolius; for while the former was alive, he bore the yoke of the life of Christ with him, and after he departed, he practiced virtue even more intensely and made many others partakers of the benefit. Thus the grace of the spiritual father was preserved in a threefold offspring, and thus the lineage became fertile, as far as virtue is concerned, for Anatolius. For twelve disciples filled his choir, breathing the same zeal for good things as he; and later, a holy house and monastery were built for him, and the man was famous and distinguished. But these things are enough for now.
XL. But we must return again to where we left off. What about the things that happened after this to the admirable Daniel, and by what miracles was his life appropriately glorified? For the emperor Leo betrothed his daughter Ariadne, who was already of marriageable age, to Zeno the Isaurian, and bestowed honor upon him by his presence at the wedding chamber.
Then he also sent him against the barbarians who were ravaging Thrace. Along with the leaders who were under his command, he went to the blessed Daniel for speedy help. And he, speaking prophetically, declared that Zeno would return unharmed, and that although some would later plot against him, he would yet prevail over their plots. These things were sufficient, and there was no need to say more, as it could be known with certainty from what had gone before that these things also happened exactly as he had said. Nevertheless, these things shall be told in detail, for the discourse is addressed to godly ears that thirst to know everything.
By this time, the emperor Leo had departed from among men, and the child born to Zeno by Ariadne, I mean the little Leo—who had also been proclaimed emperor by his grandfather—since he was, on account of his tender age, not at all suited to administer such a great empire, his father Zeno was raised to the scepter by the Senate. To this Zeno, the child, his colleague in the empire, immediately died, and the sovereignty was united solely in the father. When everything among the Romans was in peace and calm, a grave envy was kindled against him by his kinsfolk, and, to speak with the Prophet, "They hated me without a cause," and the plotters were Armatus and Basiliscus. Therefore, when Zeno frequently sensed the plots, he disregarded every other form of assistance, but went to the holy man and reported everything. And what did he not prophesy of those things that were to happen to him? And what of those things that were prophesied did not come to pass? He predicted that he would be cast out of the empire, and that he would be reared in exile, and in lack of food would eat grass, and again later be recalled by those who hated him, and return to the empire, and in it receive the end of his life. And as for him, when first those mentioned above, at the instigation of the empress Verina, were weaving plots against him, he willingly withdrew from the empire, knowing that it could not be otherwise, as night and heavy rain aided him in his flight; and having secretly crossed the strait of the sea, he arrived with his wife in Isauria.
XLI. But when the discourse reaches this point, I do not know where to turn, as many things call and draw me at the same time, which are equal in utility and equal in order. For many things were being splendidly performed by the great Daniel at the same time, and at the same time those things which he had predicted were being fulfilled; and he himself stood in the battle line for the sake of piety. Which, then, should be recounted, those or these? I think that you have already had your fill of the predictions and do not desire more, but are seeking his zeal for piety. Basiliscus, as the story goes, seized the scepter; but he immediately hissed terribly against the Church of God, and raved with some blasphemous and spurious words concerning the dispensation of the flesh assumed by Christ. As for Acacius, the one who held the high priesthood...
who indeed defended the correct doctrine but who vigorously resisted the storm of his dogmas with fervor of spirit and freedom of speech, he resolved to put him to an unjust death. This gathered together many of the monks and many of the laity as well, and the whole flock, having run together, saved the shepherd who was in danger of perishing. It had been decided, therefore, to call the great Daniel to their aid, and the priest signified the emperor’s rage against the meek man. But the emperor, who was wise at doing evil, having learned of this, himself also signifies to Daniel that this very priest was rather the cause of such turmoil. For, he says, he causes the city to rise up against me, and he subverts the military, and heaps up insults, and piles on reproaches, and employs countless things against us; for which reason it is necessary for you, he said, by your prayers rather to bring aid to the emperor against the bishop.
XLII. But here let someone consider for me the most wise discernment of the admirable Daniel, and his most noble battle for Christ; of which the former he showed in detecting the wickedness, the latter in not only heaping reproach upon him who was heaping reproach upon the humanity assumed by the true God, but also in threatening him with the greatest things, and speaking prophetically to him: "Tearing," he says, "God shall tear your kingdom," and adding other things which were able to bite and touch his heart to such a degree that not even he who was to be the minister of these words dared to announce them to the emperor by word of mouth, but asked of the great one that these things be signified to the emperor in sealed letters. And so indeed he departed. But when the high priest had gathered almost all the excellent bishops, he sends to the great Daniel, exhorting him to give his aid for Christ, saying that it was for him alone, of both tongue and virtue, to struggle against such an absurd blasphemy and to undertake the war for the Church. C These men, then, having gone, reported these things. But since it was very difficult for the holy man to descend from the pillar, and he refused, what does the patriarch contrive again? Wishing to bend him to this, he sends the bishops once more, having commanded them to appear to him in sorrow, using miserable words and gestures, and to shed tears—a most powerful medicine for persuasion—and to attempt to bend him from every direction. "Imitate," they say, "the first Master and Teacher. For in those things which are done for Christ, it is virtuous to imitate Christ. He also inclined the heavens and came down, working out the salvation of men. What, then, is absurd if you also descend for a short time from the pillar, and save the Church which is in danger of perishing, for which He whom you love did not spare His own blood? Imagine the Church herself standing by you, and letting out a cry, and extending her hands for aid."
XLIII. Having instructed the bishops with these and many other things, and having altogether exercised them so that they might...
approach the petition most fittingly and propose it most miserably; and as they did what was commanded, and cried out from below in the manner of suppliants with tears, at first the holy man was indignant at what was being done and was troubled in spirit. Afterwards, when he saw them persevering the more and praying more vehemently, sending forth hot tears with a pitiable cry, that merciful soul, torn between two conflicting thoughts, knew not what to do. For when he looked at the priests and the priests’ tears, he would be moved toward the petition; but when he looked at the pillar and the labors of those on the pillar, and the most desirable quiet, he was inflexible. So, with his thoughts thus conflicting and standing as it were in equilibrium, he sought the inclination of divine judgment; and as he stood in prayer, a voice forthwith proceeded from heaven, commanding him both to descend and to return again to the pillar. Thus, therefore, the bishops, receiving him with joy, led him to the high priest; and it is impossible to describe how full of joy he was again, and with how much honor he received him. Straightway, then, the soldier of Christ turned back his adversary, though he did not appear before him, but used only words as if they were javelins cast from afar, just as he had done before through the letter he had sent, calling him a new Diocletian; and by terrifying him with both—the punishments of the present age and of the future—he persuaded that wicked slave to flee. For, fearing the threats and giving the answer, "Behold, I concede to you the city, and all who are in it," he himself departed outside the borders of the city under the pretext of exercise.
XLIV. But not even then did the most valiant man let him go; but he pursued the fugitive as a soldier who had cast away his shield, seeking to take him for examination and to engage with him in single combat. He went, therefore, by the road that led there. But see for me the zeal of the athlete. For when his feet had suffered from the exercise and could not walk, he borrowed others’ feet and was led along, being carried by others. Was his journey, then, empty and devoid of wonders? By no means; and most gladly would I have recorded them all, but since three things hinder—the length of the narrative, the weakness of my power of speech, and that I might not, by wishing to tell all, make you weary and sluggish in the things that are most important—I shall pass over most things and mention a few. A certain leper approaching him on the road, uttered a voice, which one formerly uttered to Christ—save that he called him Son, but this man called him the servant of God—and he asked to obtain healing. And leaving Him who is able to heal these things, have you approached a man who is subject to similar affections, asking for things that exceed his strength? "Nevertheless, if you believe that not only He, but also His servants are able to heal the same, there shall be healing for you through us also, as you have believed." As he then confessed these things and still more earnestly asked for the healing, pity and at the same time wonder entered the great man—pity for the disease, and wonder for the fervor of his faith; and turning, [he made] signs...
signaled to those who followed, that they should also lift their hands to heaven and appease the Divine with tears. Then, at his command, having washed his body in the sea and been cleansed (for it was not far off), he was shown to those present entirely whole. When they saw what had happened and received the healed man naked, what did they not do, what did they not say, what did they not feel? They marveled, they shouted, they gave glory, they were struck with amazement. Faith moved their tongue to thanksgiving, but wonder checked their speech and rendered them speechless in their astonishment. And, indeed, they had no words to say—whether concerning his faith, or the other's grace, or the power of God. All eyes were fixed on him alone, and there was one voice and one story: the leper and his cleansing. In order that there might be more witnesses to this paradox, they arrive at the greatest temple of God and place the man upon a raised platform, doing as Zacchaeus did. For faith persuaded him, too, to climb the sycamore tree; and a similar fervor of the Spirit persuaded these men, in turn, to place the healed man on high. Who could tell the things that happened then on account of the great Daniel?
XLV.
Almost the entire city streams toward him—those who were sick, out of a desire for healing, having the example close at hand in the healed leper; and the healthy, to see those who were being healed and not to miss the sight of the miracles. And miracles, greater than can be expressed in words or perceived by the ears, followed the faith of those who approached. But when that great man, being escorted in such splendor, came near to where the Emperor was, close to the palaces of the Septimum, a certain man, a Goth by race, having looked out from a window above, and having thus seen the holy man being escorted and carried, mocks him for his weakness and ironically exclaims, "Behold," he says, "the new consul." He said these things, and immediately he received the fruit of his mockery; for he was cast down from there and lay dead upon the ground. When the bodyguards saw what had happened, and were filled with grief and at the same time with anger, and furthermore were seized with fear lest some other among those about them, or the Emperor himself, should fare similarly by speaking some such thing against the great man, they would not allow the holy man to enter the palace. When, however, those with him tried many times to enter, and there was a great commotion, the holy man then encouraged those around him to shake off the dust from their feet according to the Gospel, and having been the first to do this, he was returning. Yet he was not empty-handed even then; for many of the soldiers, having been struck by his angelic manner and appearance, even if only for a short while, immediately followed him as they were. Thus, then, the man of God departed for Byzantium.
XLVI.
The Emperor, having been awed, as it were, by the man’s virtue, and fearing lest some grave harm should come to him because of the slight shown toward him, sends and begs him to return as quickly as possible. But he, not only not softened, but
even more provoked, said that he who tries to exacerbate the heavenly King stores up much greater evils for himself; and he added that before long he would fall from his reign, and would not escape the threats from above. Having said these things, he shook from his tunic any dust that might have remained, and departed with a confident heart. While these things were being reported to the Emperor, the tower of the palace fell down of its own accord, just as if God wished to instruct the arrogant and insolent man through inanimate things. But as for what miracles were performed by the holy man as he arrived (for just as they followed him going out of the city, so too they followed him as he returned, with the power of God, as it were, escorting its servant, so to speak), having brought forward a few of these, for the sake of completeness, I will pass on to what remains.
XLVII. Two young men who were vexed by demons were first healed by him. Then another woman, of Canaanite faith, whose daughter was afflicted, and who was pouring forth from her eyes tears that were witnesses to her inflamed soul, and was seeking healing through them, received her daughter back in health, and having obtained joy instead of grief, she returned home. Then, as the holy man was moving through the middle of the city, a certain patrician from the senate, who himself also imitated Zacchaeus, having received him into his house with fervent faith, also obtained a similar blessing. But after he was in the very great temple of God, and was staying together with the patriarch who had received him with great joy and honor, even here he performed miracles novel to others, but customary to himself. A serpent, which had crawled from somewhere and had wrapped itself in its coils around the feet of the great Daniel, was a terrible sight; and when those who were present had rushed up to defend him, he reproached them for their timidity, while he himself, having threatened the serpent boldly and confidently, and having commanded it with great authority as if it were a servant: "Go," he says, "and betake yourself to your usual den and your subterranean hiding-places." And it, as if unable to gainsay him, immediately crawls to the wall and bursts asunder in the sight of all.
XLVIII. When the woman Rais had learned these things about the great Daniel—a woman of most fervent and pious faith, and of very notable glory—she ran to him with a ready and eager mind, and in like manner to the harlot, she moistened his feet with her tears and entreated him with prayers that a son would be born to her. But when, cast down upon the ground, she saw the sole of his foot split from the practice of the tendon, she was amazed at his endurance and uttered words similar to the Canaanite woman. For, giving him a small cord, she asked that after he had wrapped it around his foot, he would return it to her, crying out that this was the sum of her petition. At first he did not consent; afterwards, however, moved by her tears, and besides bent by the words of the bishop and the others, he not only returned the cord to her after having wrapped it around his foot, but also signified that she would bear a son, and he foretold the
son's name, saying, "O woman, you shall call him Zeno." Such, then, was the holy man. But I will say nothing more; for it is clear that this is what happened, since nothing whatever failed of what was predicted by him. Because of the holy man, the emperor was afraid, and as he was altogether unwilling to oppose him, he attempted to win him over by artifice and flattery. And since he could not persuade him to come to him, even after having asked him many times and through many people, he himself came to him; and in the guise of a servant he made obeisance, and laid hold of his feet, asking, as he pretended, to be granted forgiveness. But the saint (for who was more clear-sighted than he to see through such things?) then upbraided him with many reproaches. Later, he also revealed what was hidden from the many, saying that this humility and contrition was an artificial simulation, covering over, under a shaggy sheepskin, his severity and savagery; but that he would soon see the eye of God, which sees all things, and His mighty hand, which casts down even the powerful. And when the saint had thus performed such great miracles, and had turned back and routed the enemy of God, and had settled the affairs of the Church well, and had even prophesied future events, he returned again to the column, and with a great and brave spirit took up his struggles and labors.
XLIX. Not much time passed, and Basiliscus was driven from the kingdom, as the prophecy of Daniel had foretold, and Zeno again held the scepter; and his predictions received their fulfillment. Zeno came to the great Daniel with his wife, seeing that the outcomes of the predictions were coming to pass, and intending to offer thanks worthy of the events. Now, as my discourse is seeking its end and desires to rest along with the life of the great man, let us dwell a little longer on his miracles. A man who was a goldsmith by trade had a child who had already reached his seventh year, yet was not able to walk, but used his belly to move about, after the manner of creeping things. His parents took him and placed him before the column, and they themselves, while wailing piteously and tearfully below, provoked even those who saw them to tears. The saint, looking down from above, having quieted their grief, partly by rebuking them and partly by exhorting them, bade them persevere in the holy house of Symeon and make use of the venerable relics, and to apply these as a kind of release to the feet that were bound by the disease. He spoke, and it was done. And on the seventh day, when the prayer was being performed by the blessed one as was the custom (O Creator, worker of wonders!), they immediately see the child leaping, and climbing the steps of the column, and embracing the column.
L. Another man, traveling from the East, fell among robbers. They, having inflicted stripes upon him—to use the expression of Holy Scripture—took what he was carrying; and furthermore, having cut the sinews of his knees, they left him half-dead. Some travelers, having seen him lying there in this state, kindly picked him up and carried him to the neighboring city of Ancyra, and informed the bishop of the city of this, from whom
he was handed over to the care of many physicians. And although he received great attention from them, his wounds were healed, but he did not at all recover the ability to walk. For Daniel had not, in truth, yet grasped this holy anchor. Therefore, having persuaded the bishop with many tears to send him to the great Daniel (for he swore that this was the cause for which he had set out on his journey), he was carried to the saint like inanimate cargo placed upon beasts of burden. Then, having been placed at the pillar, what did he not say to move him to pity? What great lamentations did he not offer? He cried out, he wept, he pleaded with his hands, he recalled to [the saint's] memory with his eyes the journey he had undertaken on his account, and showed the evils he had suffered at the hands of the robbers. But here, consider with me how great a man he was, how averse to pride, and how he hated glory; and how, in fleeing honor, he applied as much effort as others do with greed to obtain glory. For so that others might attribute the healing to someone else, he sent them to the oratory of Symeon, even if the glory returned to him even more abundantly because of this. But we must return to where we left off. For after he had been carried to the temple of Symeon, as he had been ordered, and was anointed daily with the oil taken from the relics, it was now the sixth day near dawn. As the great Daniel was standing for the morning chants, the same thing happened to this man as to the previous one; for suddenly he stood upon his feet, and ran up the steps, and embracing the pillar, he gave thanks to God and His servant. But why do I not mention something greater? A certain Hippasius, a centurion in authority and faith, just like the one celebrated in the Gospels—this man, then, if ever he saw any of his relatives, kin, children, or servants afflicted by disease, would seek only a written word from the saint. By placing the letter upon the sick person, he would immediately put the disease to flight as if it were a fugitive. Having mentioned one more [miracle], I shall proceed to the departure of the great man.
LI. A certain woman, having a son twelve years old who had been mute from birth—she, I say, having entered secretly and deposited her beloved child in the saint’s monastery, departed with good hope. The monks therefore saw the boy, and having taken him, they led him to the saint. And he, having only seen him, ordered him to stay with them, to be a servant of God. But after they signified to the blessed one that the boy was mute, he ordered the child’s tongue to be anointed with the oil of the saints. Who could recount the things that followed thereafter? They suspected that the mother, out of poverty, had falsely claimed her son was mute so that the boy might be fed there. And approaching him, some tried to strike terror into him so that, being disturbed, he might speak. Others would prick the sleeping child with needles or sharp sticks, and suddenly rouse him from his sleep, so that he might cry out or speak something and be discovered. But he, although he himself wished to speak out of pain, had his tongue bound by the strong chain of his affliction.
was mute, speaking much to God with the mouth of his heart. The first and foremost of days arrived, and as the deacon was about, according to custom, to read the Gospel of Christ, when he first announced the name of the evangelist to the people, the boy, forestalling everyone, said with a pure and very loud voice, "Glory to you, O Lord"; and thenceforth, through the whole of the sacred ministry, he continued to chant and respond together with the people. Many other things, indeed, were accomplished by the great Daniel, which can neither be explained by words nor—for fear of satiety—contained by the ears.
LII. But these things will suffice to show how rich he was in boldness toward God, and how great an intimacy he had with Him. I will say this one thing only, and as it were seal what has been said: that along with many and very great miracles, so great was his gentleness and moderation—and not merely moderation, but, as I may say with the divine David 10, contrition and supreme humility—that one would not admire him more for the working of miracles than for the fact that, while performing such things, he considered that he had nothing more than others. Nay, even while he excelled so greatly in virtue, he was exceedingly cautious and sparing when it came to judging the things of others. And in everything regarding God and matters beyond us, his teaching was so fearful and guarded that when, on a certain occasion, some people were prying into theology and proposing—allegedly with the backing of priests—certain absurdities, and were causing him trouble, he would say: "If the contention is about God, the choir of the apostles and the voices of the Fathers who theologized are sufficient for you, and there is no need to inquire further; but if you have a doubt about human affairs and priests, all these things must be left to the judgment of God and to those who have received such an administration from Him. As for us, it is enough to be shepherded by others and to look to our own affairs, rather than to cling to these superfluous and harmful questions." And these things are thus.
LIII. Now that the discourse has reached the end of the saint, it too is about to lapse into silence. But having said a few small things here before the end, I shall thus conclude the account. That prophetic man knew, along with all the other things, his own departure to the Lord, and he openly foretold this to his disciples. And when the appointed day was already drawing near, he left them a blessing in writing, just as some others leave their estates, and a scribe served him; such was his testament: "I," he says, "O children and brothers (for you are both: the one because I begot you spiritually, the other because God is the common Father of all), I am now going to this common Father. But I will not leave those dear to me as orphans, lamenting the absence of a father, but I leave them to the care of that Father who created me with you all. He, therefore, who fashions all things...
by His Word and wisdom, and then bowed the heavens and descended to the earth, and for our sake died and rose again, He himself shall be with you; as a wise one, guarding you from the evil one; as a Master, preserving you for His own will; and as a Father, if you should stumble in any way, kindly calling you back and spiritually spreading His arms to you; and as one who surrendered Himself to death for our sake, He shall join you to one another in harmony and ensure that you are one in the Spirit. Pursue humility, serve with obedience, love hospitality, fasting, vigil, poverty, and that which is the first and greatest of the commandments, love; hold fast to those things that pertain to piety, beware of the tares of the heretics, and do not be separated in the least from your mother, the Church. If you do all these things, you will be perfect in virtue.
LIV. These things he dictated with pious lips and ordered to be read to all the monks standing at the steps of the ladder and lamenting his departure.
