It is said, moreover, that this vision was seen concerning him: namely, three days before his death, at the middle of the night, all the saints who have existed from the ages—prophets, apostles, martyrs, and finally all the holy ones—assembled and came to him, and they greeted the great man kindly and bade him celebrate the divine mystery. This he indeed celebrated, and he himself received the immaculate sacraments and imparted them to those for whom it was fitting. And already the great man was breathing his last, and the chief of the bishops himself (this was Euphemius, for Acacius was already dead) was present with others for his departure. C Also present was the most faithful Rais, and a certain man who was possessed by a demon was approaching the column; he openly signified by shouting the arrival of the saints to the holy man, and he called them by their names, and he also made mention of the angels who had arrived. Then he even added that at the third hour of this day Daniel himself would depart to the Lord, and the unclean spirit would be driven out from its dwelling of long duration. Both of these things came to pass at their proper time. D The previously mentioned Rais, however, was magnificently preparing the things that pertained to the burial; and having brought many craftsmen and having ordered a tomb to be made in the lower part of the base of the column all the way to its summit, she stationed men on either side of the tomb, who carried torches and held lamps in their hands and sang the chants that are customary at a passing. And when she had brought the body down, she placed it in a leaden coffin, preserving in this also the testament of the great Daniel. For he had commanded his disciples: "Whether emperors or any others contend with one another to surpass each other in the cost and magnificence of the coffin, do not accept it, but allow the body to be placed there and in such a way as the most faithful Rais may wish." These things, therefore, were accomplished, and the unclean demon was driven out after he had greatly rent the man.
LV. Furthermore, some other miracles were also accomplished. Three
comes. After this the emperor, having sent for what had been requested, ordered three temples to be built: two, indeed, which the saint had desired, and a third according to the will of the archdeacon Theodulus, likewise consecrated to the Wisdom of God. When these temples had been constructed, the holy Amphilochius summoned Basil the Great to inaugurate those newly built temples. He, although he was then seized by illness, yet, proving stronger than it, arrived and consecrated the temples, and there was spiritual joy and a hovering of the Holy Spirit. Two years having passed after this, the Trinity called the great Basil to the temple not made by hands. After the holy Amphilochius had consigned his sacred body to the earth, he returned home and performed the office of teaching just as he was accustomed to do before. Finally, three years after the death of the great Basil, he also departed to the Lord, to receive the reward of his long struggles.
IX. The city, however, as is wont to happen in a public calamity, being gathered together, did not easily endure the passing of its pastor, but, seized by grief, was poured out in wailings and tears, insomuch that all wished they had breathed their last along with their pastor. When, therefore, they had honorably buried that truly venerable body and shed many tears, they could scarcely be brought to leave the tomb. Many miracles were performed there by God, who knows how to glorify those who glorify Him; to Whom is due all glory, honor, and adoration, with the only-begotten Son and the most holy Spirit, unto the ages. Amen.
MONTH OF DECEMBER.
LIFE AND CONDUCT OF OUR HOLY FATHER DANIEL THE STYLITE.
(Latin in Surius for the 11th of December, Greek from ms. cod. 1559, 14th century.)
I. Just as it happens with brave men, that when someone talks about wars and struggles and trophies,
and they hear more words upon these subjects, their desire increases the more, and they immediately wish to undertake such deeds, so likewise with the lovers of virtue: if anyone sets before them lives that are practiced in monastic exercise and that pass through every virtue that reconciles one to God, they are at once inflamed, and they desire the same things, and generally they are disposed in a way appropriate to what is said, and they prepare themselves for imitation. For how is the life of this admirable Daniel not alone most sufficient to inflame a soul that loves virtue toward greater zeal, and altogether to move and soothe it, and to bring the pleasure of the exercise of the most sweet labors, which has no satiety? For in what it is more admirable, in that it is also more useful; and if anyone wishes to emulate it, he will find in a small thing much virtue, and that which can be compared with few things.
II. B To this blessed man, then, the fatherland was that which lies between the Euphrates and the Tigris, and it is and is called Mesopotamia. He was born in a certain village, a neighbor to the city of Samosata; the local language calls it Maratha. To him the father was Eliu, and the mother Martha; the manner of conception was this. The mother’s womb was terribly maimed and disabled, and she was sterile with regard to the procreation of children. Since, therefore, her husband, acquaintances, kinsfolk, and all others reproached her every day with a thousand insults and bitterly cast her lack of children in her face, she, being evilly treated by them and tormented in her heart, what does she do? She flees to Him who is able to heal these things; and, slipping out of the house secretly in the middle of the night, lifting her hands to heaven and watering the earth with tears: "Lord King," she said, "who from the beginning created male and female, who said, 'Increase and multiply' (Gen. I, 28); who gave Isaac to Sarah, and Samuel to Anna, and John to Elizabeth; do Thou also pity me in a similar calamity, and look with compassion, and loosen this sterility, and take away the reproach, and give me the fruit of a barren womb, that I may offer this also to Thee, the One who gave it, just as the former Anna [offered] Samuel." Having cried out so bitterly in contrition of spirit, she returns inside the house, and having slept a little, she sees in a dream two luminaries, excellent in beauty, immense in magnitude, and in shape like a disk. She seemed to see them leaving heaven and gently descending to hover above her head. When she rose in the morning and related the vision to her husband and to her kin, one person said one thing would happen, another said another; but it was in reality a prelude of Him who was to shine forth from her brighter than the stars.
III. Not much time passed, and having conceived, she brought forth the blessed Daniel, and by the birth she is at once freed of both, namely, of the sterility and of the distress of mind. Such were for the blessed Daniel the preludes of his life; thus he came
into the light, the true offspring of light. But the boy had now reached his fifth year, yet no name had been imposed upon him by his parents, as they wished this too to be given to him by God, just as his transition into life had proceeded from Him. The parents therefore led the boy to a monastery; at the same time, they also brought whatever pious hands are accustomed to offer to God, so that here, in a truly holy place, he might receive his name. When they had arrived and were asking the superior of the spiritual fold to assign another name to the child, that divine man, having become as one inspired, said that the name to be bestowed should be whatever the Lord would absolutely reveal. And looking at the child—at this very one, I mean, Daniel—he ordered one of the books lying on the table to be brought (for it was their custom for books to lie upon the holy altar for the common benefit of the monks). Immediately, therefore, the boy did what was commanded, and he brought a book, not, as one might say, by simple chance, but by divine counsel and guidance, bringing the one that contained the oracles of the prophet Daniel. Having judged, therefore, as if this were a sign from heaven, the man immediately called him by the same name as the prophet, as he was similar to him in character; and as it pleased God, he called the child Daniel.
IV. Now if this divine man had yielded to the words of the parents, then Daniel would have been immediately dedicated to God and from his earliest hair counted among the monks. But as he was not persuaded, but rather pointed out his age and that he was still a mere infant, they returned home with Daniel, it seeming better to the Superior that the boy should choose the good not by the decision of others, but by his own. Immediately, then, the first sprout indicated what sort of plant it would be, and the shadows of virtues already followed him who walked through the light. But with his age advancing a little, he clearly showed how great a growth of spirit he had attained beyond his years. For when Daniel had just touched upon his twelfth year, and without having said anything to anyone or revealing the desire of his soul, he despised his parents for the sake of Christ, and he despised his kin, and the world, and all that is in the world, and, having left all things—friends, and that village which bore him—he arrived at a coenobium of monks about twelve stadia away from it. Falling at the feet of the superior, he asked to be enrolled among them and to be clothed in the monastic habit. But when he, pointing out the tenderness of his body and his youth, said that it was not possible for him to undergo the life of monks, for he would soon be exhausted by the labors, which are many and difficult even for men—vigils, sleeping on the ground, fastings, the perfect mortification of the body and its desires—and therefore urged him to remain for some time with his parents and not to undertake such labors at an immature age and with a body not yet robust and adult: "I, for my part," said Daniel, "have come for this very reason, that I might thus live for Christ, and die to the flesh D
and every other bodily hardship; as I saw these things, the others..."
V. When, therefore, the superior of the holy monastery had said many things and had also heard much, but could not blunt his impulsion nor his keen love, he took the brothers into counsel as to whether it behoved him to be admitted into their monastery. But as they were struck by the boy’s great constancy and the intensity of his spirit’s zeal, and surmised that the thing moving him was divine, and they were glad to consent to his reception, from that time onward Daniel was with them; and was indeed above them in his life according to God and his fervour for virtue. A short time intervened, and when his parents learned where he was and with whom, they came to him with admiration. But when they had seen that the boy had dedicated and consecrated himself to God, they felt nothing of a human or paternal nature; nor did they ask to take back their dearest son and return home with him, and this even though he was not yet fully initiated into the monastic habit. Rather, truly wishing to show themselves to be his parents, they rejoiced greatly at what had come to pass, and with many supplications entreated the superior of the monastery to clothe him in the monastic garment before their very eyes. He again communicated this matter to the monks, and after he saw that they too agreed, he commanded everyone to be gathered in the Lord’s house, and having recited the holy rule for Daniel, he sheared his hair and clothed him in the monastic habit, as he was already living and was resolved to live like a monk. Having instructed his parents not to make frequent visits to the boy, he sends them away rejoicing, which is indeed a very strange thing, parents deprived of their son.
VI. The blessed Daniel was therefore there, progressing and growing strong in spirit; and as he grew, all the seeds of virtue grew within him as well, and others too followed not long after. But that which was for him the beginning of a more excellent life, and the root of the fruits that afterwards appeared, this I shall now proceed to tell. Divine love kindled his heart and ignited an intense flame of desire to see those places in which Christ endured the passion for us, and where He was buried, and from whence, having risen, He ascended into the heavens. At the same time, a certain divine longing also inflamed his soul to behold that man, great in exercise—I mean Simeon, who was upon the pillar. Approaching the superior of the monastery, he communicated to him what he desired and begged that what he asked not be denied to him. The superior did not indeed yield of his own accord at that time; but not long after, he permitted it, albeit reluctantly. For as it was necessary for him to go to Antioch because of ecclesiastical cares, he then took Daniel, along with certain other of the brothers, and began the journey. And when they had arrived at the village which is called Telada, where that great man of holiness, Simeon, was performing those great labours of exercise—and when they had arrived at the very pillar, he was gazing at and... D
considering the height of the column, the harshness of the place, and how that noble man endured the cold of winter and the heat of summer, the threats of storms, the force of winds, and every other physical challenge. When others—the more foolish among them—saw this, they imagined the saint was undergoing such great struggles out of vainglory. But blessed Daniel was not only struck with amazement, he was also stirred to imitation and received a sting of holy zeal in his soul. And when those below had called out, and that great man, looking down from above, told them to set a ladder and climb up to him, it was then a fine opportunity to witness an exact proof of their souls; for one, pleading the weakness of his feet, another his kidneys, another his old age, and others something else, declined the ascent. But Daniel, having run and begged for the teacher’s permission, and being urged on by him, climbed up with eagerness and greeted the great Simeon with joy and wonder.
VII. Indeed, having been deemed worthy of a blessing from him, as was fitting, through the laying on of hands, he also received a prophecy of what was to come. For he heard: “Take courage, child, and let your heart be strengthened. For you are about to endure many and grievous labours for the sake of Christ, who will also invigorate your soul to bear them nobly.” Daniel then descended after this sweet conversation with the man and returned to this holy monastery. When God called the superior of the monastery to Himself, this excellent Daniel—who had recently learned how to be well shepherded and was now being considered as capable of shepherding others well, and was being forcibly dragged by his fellow monks to do so—he, desiring his beloved silence, and not only knowing that he should by no means yield to their words but also suggesting to them another who would profitably oversee them, was meanwhile counselling himself with the best advice, saying, “Look, Daniel, you have become free and beholden to no one. Behold the time you desired long ago. Hold fast, then, to your purpose and your way, and make haste to bring to completion that which you have set before yourself.” Thus having left everything behind, and having disclosed the secret of his soul to no one, he left the monastery in secret and reached that enclosure where the pillar of blessed Simeon was situated; for underneath, around it, there was a certain monastery. When he had spent fourteen whole days there, Simeon still wanted Daniel to remain with him (for like is delighted by like), but he could not bear it, as he was held by another desire. For he longed to see the holy places and was searching for a deeper desert; but hearing that the road toward Palestine was difficult and impassable, inasmuch as the Samaritans were attacking the Christians by law of war, he, not able to endure slackening his zeal, having strengthened himself and persuaded himself to despise death, was ready to accomplish what he was intent upon.
VIII. And since he had begun his journey, there met him on the way a man, of more than elderly age, of the monastic
habit, with gray hair, and in all things resembling Symeon who was on the pillar. Addressing Daniel therefore in the Syriac language, and embracing him, he asked what his journey was and where it led. He replied, "If it seems good to God, I am going to the holy places." The other, taking him up, said, "You have spoken well, 'If it seems good to God.' But now know clearly that what you have proposed is not of God's will; for have you not heard of the sedition and tumult which is in Palestine?" He answered, "I have heard, but I trust in God that He will be my helper; and indeed I mostly hope that nothing evil at all will happen to us. But if something of the sort should happen, 'whether we live or whether we die, we are the Lord's'." When the old man made this prophetic response: "May He not permit your foot to be moved, and He who keeps you shall not slumber, the angel," and Daniel had answered this with a ready and eager spirit, saying that it was altogether good to die for Christ, the gray-haired man, bearing it with difficulty, turned away and said, "I do not allow it. For God does not permit us to throw ourselves before the time into death and force our own destruction. He is the One who said, 'If they persecute you from this city, flee into another'." When the saint had yielded to the objection and said, "If it seems right to you, I do not delay my return," the other said, "I do not advise you to abandon this good endeavor entirely—for that I would be foolish—but if you trust me, bidding a long farewell to the East, turn and journey to Byzantium, a second Jerusalem—or rather indeed the first. For its privileges have now been given to it by God, in which it will be permitted you to enjoy many temples and many sacred spectacles; and if it is your heart's desire to live in quiet and silence, either in Upper Thrace or at the very mouth of the Pontus, there will be great opportunity for you in this matter, and God will fulfill your desire. Furthermore, you ought not to think that you will encounter God in Jerusalem, but not in Byzantium; for the Divine is by no means circumscribed by place, my beloved."
IX. As the gray-haired man was holding these conversations with Daniel, the sun was already at settings, and it happened that they turned aside to a certain monastery. When Daniel had heard that they were to lodge there: "You, old man, go in first," said he; "I will follow you at your heels." Thinking therefore that the saint was urged by some bodily necessity, he entered the gate and waited. But after he was no longer present, Daniel, thinking that he had lodged somewhere else, approached the prior of the monastery alone and gave to him and the brothers under him an honest kiss, the sign of love. Then he also partook of the food set before him. But when he knew it was the time for sleep, the old man standing by him again urged him to do those things that he had said before, and to reach Byzantium as soon as possible. And he, being awakened, was pondering who this might be, and whether he was some man or one of the angels. But he was the great one
commanding him to reach the queen of cities, that this path might also become for many a way toward salvation. Having therefore decided to do so without saying a word to anyone, Daniel, after he had sung the nightly hymns, having bidden farewell to all and departed, set out with haste toward Constantinople. And upon reaching the mouth of the Pontus, the temple of the prince of the host, Michael, which is beautifully situated there, receives him; and he spent seven days there. But having heard some people speaking of another temple, which was situated below the one called the holy [temple], and the place was named Philemporos by the inhabitants, and having learned that evil spirits dwelt in it—and that they sometimes drowned those sailing by, and attacked those passing through, and were evil neighbors to those dwelling nearby, and that it was impossible for anyone to pass by there unharmed, not in the morning, nor in the evening, nor at midday—taking into his mind Antony the Great and his affairs, and how many temptations he too had endured from the demons, and how he had in the end prevailed over them all by the power of the Lord, and what honors he was deemed worthy of having prevailed, he too girded himself for the struggle. And having asked those who were present to show him the temple, and having reached the vestibule, he finds himself in the middle of the temple, carrying as an impregnable weapon the cross of Christ, and chanting at the same time: "The Lord is my light and my savior; whom shall I fear?" (Ps. XXVI, 1) Wherefore, having struck terror into the very phalanx of the demons even before the engagement, he went around every corner, searching for the fleeing enemies, and on each of these [spots] performing genuflections.
X.
It was now evening, and the prince of darkness was present, and stones were being hurled at Daniel, and a sound was heard, and a confused roar and clamor were stirred up. But he, persevering in prayer, was once again unmoved. Then he also [spoke] to them: "If an army should encamp against me," he said, "my heart shall not fear." (Ps. XXVI, 3) He passed the first night and the second, which followed, in this way. On the third, he saw men of greatest stature, strange in form, and in cruelty, as one might conjecture, flesh-eaters, horribly gnashing [their teeth] and threatening Daniel, and mixing their defense with fear, saying: "Who, O wretched one, persuaded you to reach this place? Since—if nothing else—the time we have stayed here already shows us to be masters of the place." And some of them threatened to cast him into the sea, while others again kept hurling huge stones; yet no one dared to draw near to him. What then does he do? He obeys the Master, who said that this race is driven out only by fasting and prayer. And first, he himself threatened them in return, for so much did he trust in the power of Christ; then, as he saw them acting even more shamelessly and causing a clamor, having utterly despised them, he blocked up the entire entrance of the temple, showing that he alone wished against those alone
to wrestle, and that he did not even need to flee when they attacked, but only opened a small window for the sake of seeing those who approached, and for receiving meager nourishment for the body. Not much time passed, and by the power of Christ all that wicked activity of the demons was destroyed; the whole place was rendered free from their assaults, and henceforth all walked about and sailed the sea freely and without any snares.
XI. The fame of this matter spread everywhere, and it was possible to see men and women together, young and old, the poor and the rich, and all who lived in the surrounding regions, being carried toward him as if by a continuous stream, and marveling at how, where previously there was destruction, there was now salvation, and where there were the shoutings of demons, there night and day were sung songs to God. But the envious Satan, the enemy of mankind, unable to tolerate seeing his own defeat, or the patience of the holy man, or the benefit of the pious, set about a second craftiness, and he breathed into some of those in the church who had been enrolled in the clergy such thoughts as were formerly held by the Jews against Christ: that it was not good to allow a man to live here of whom you do not know whence he is, and who draws everyone to himself by his fame, and whose honor is entirely a diminution of your revenues. "But," he said, "make these things known to the bishop, and he will quickly expel him." And this was the sower, and such was his seed. Those who received it were known by no other sign than that they immediately brought these things forth. As soon as they received the seed, they produced the fruit: and they approached the bishop and reported these things about the holy man. The bishop of Constantinople (this was Anatolius), said, "And how, tell me, do you speak evil of a man and attempt to expel him when you neither know whence he is nor what manner of life he leads? On the contrary, it is necessary to let him stay here, so that if he should be a good man, he may make us also partakers of the benefit; but if the opposite, he himself will justly be expelled from here, and you and I will be free from blame in this matter." Thus, they ensnared by themselves—for vice of this kind falls into itself, and is caught by its own deeds—covering their faces in shame, ceased from their wickedness.
XII. But when the wicked one saw that this snare of his had also been dissolved, it would be a long story to tell how many visions, more grievous than the former, he again brought upon the blessed man, and how many molestations and vexations, now threatening that he would kill him, now to drown him in the sea, now to do some other thing of the sort which he did not wish, even if he himself and his evil host were to suffer these things most of all from him. For after he had risen and prayed, and had spoken boldly, saying: "The Lord
Jesus Christ, in whom you have believed and I believe, may He cast you into the lowest abyss." Then they, wailing bitterly and crying out lamentably, and seeming to strike their own faces, having become like bats, departed and went away while the saint himself was watching.
He, however, never ceasing from doing harm, thinking that if he should attack him frequently, as in a siege, he would eventually be able to depress and break the noble spirit of the blessed one, proceeds to a third trial of malice and again stirs up his former calumniators against him. These, having approached the bishop, as if they had already suffered the greatest injuries, called the holy man a sorcerer, an impostor, and a deceiver, and said that his company was intolerable to them, as he was burdensome and troublesome even by his mere appearance. And he, having by then been overcome by the vexations, immediately sends for Daniel and brings him before him, and asks who he is, and from where, and of what things he is in need, and, above all, what opinion he holds concerning the Divine that he settled in these parts. But he, having first made mention of the greatest and first of the orthodox faith in Christ, then strung together the rest—his country, his lineage, and finally that a divine revelation had moved him to see Byzantium and to arrive here. When the bishop heard these things, he rose up and embraced him, and honored him as truly a man of God, and rebuked those who were speaking against him. And these things, to be sure, were thus well-conducted by the great one; but it was not at all possible for one who had undertaken the life of Christ not also to appear imitating the order of His life as much as possible. Just as then his Master appears after the temptation to be beginning His signs, so too does this one, except in so far as He was moving him, and he, by His power, was performing the miracles.
XIII. Not many days passed, and a severe illness seized the patriarch, which even caused him to become a suppliant to the holy man, and he sends for him immediately, and asks him to pray to Christ on his behalf, to bring help to him in his distress. But when he had merely bent his knees in prayer, Christ was present, hearing him, almost anticipating the petition; and the illness abated, and the high priest became sound. And so much for the miracle; yet one would not be more astonished by the healing than by the kindness shown in the healing. For after he had healed him, he asked for a reward for the healing; and this was that the debt be remitted to those who had slandered him. The pontiff gladly granted his request and said, "For how could I not remit it to those to whom I am almost giving thanks as well, because they brought it about that I should see you, who have bestowed salvation upon me, and were the cause that you should come to us?" But when he reminded him of a monastery, and of gifts, and that he should stay with him always, "If you wish," says the great one, "to follow me with great zeal, allow me"
to do those things which seem right to God and to me." And so, being sent to the temple with a great and illustrious retinue, when he had done the same things as before and had blocked the entrance, he would speak through a small hole to those who approached. Nine years meanwhile had passed, and Providence, calling him to a more perfect life, openly showed him things to come. For when he had been in an ecstasy, he saw a pillar of cloud, and it was raised up very high, and it hung in the air opposite him; and the admirable Simeon was standing upon the top of the pillar. Two youths stood by him, and their garment was bright and shining. Then from above, from the top of the pillar, he heard a voice saying, "Hurry to me, Daniel, and ascend." But when he replied, "And how, O Lord, shall I be able to be raised to such a height?" Simeon ordered the youths who were with him to descend and, having taken him up, to bring him to himself. They performed what was commanded, and they lifted him up and placed him beside him. And he, having immediately embraced him, lifted him up at once into the sky, and he cried out with a loud voice: "Be strong, Daniel; show yourself sturdy and robust, and stand beautifully and generously." But after a very clear voice rang around him (for it seemed to be terrible and like thunder), Daniel, having had his hearing struck and being disturbed, returned as it were from a dream.
XIV. This prospectively heralded his future way of life according to Simeon, and that it behooved him both to ascend a pillar and to move to a place much higher than the pillar. Thus were all things accomplished for him by the counsel and will of the Almighty, and not long after, the sheepskin of Elias was sent to him—to him, I say, who in his ways was like Elisha. And it was sent in this way: Sergius, his disciple, had been sent by Simeon to the emperor, bringing him a gift that wards off all evils, namely, the covering which he wore on his head (it is customary to call this a cowl). But the emperor having his mind fixed on other things and being occupied with public cares, while God was managing and dispensing all these things, the aforementioned Sergius decided to sail back and hastened to reach the monastery which they name the Acoemetae, that is, the "non-sleeping" ones. When, therefore, he was sailing back and was approaching the place of Daniel with some others, immediately, as if by a signal, the blessed one was on everyone's lips. And some remembered his endurance, others his prayers, others his cures, and others that he had made mild a place which had been possessed by demons; and that now those who are on the sea sail without danger, and those who are on foot go on their journey unharmed. Sergius, to whom the most precious gift had been entrusted, learned these things, and his soul being moved toward desire, he inquired who this was, and where, and if it were possible to meet him. They pointed out the place to him with alacrity, and when he had landed on the shore, they themselves arrived together with Sergius to the holy man. He received the blessed man very kindly, and he himself explained the departure of Simeon, as I said, from these parts, and what gift he was bringing from him to the emperor. "Rather to you…
Daniel," he said, "was this sent than to him to whom I thought it was being dispatched." But he heard other things in return from Daniel, which God had revealed to him concerning Simeon and the life to be lived in accordance with his rule. When Sergius heard these things, he immediately set aside the sacred gift and bestowed it upon Daniel, saying that it was passing from Father Simeon to Father Daniel. And these things happened in this way.
XV. God, doing as He was accustomed, again stirred up Daniel through a vision that was more divine, that he might undertake a way of life greater and more sublime, foreknowing him to be a chosen vessel, as Paul was. The vision appeared not to Daniel, but rather to Sergius. For it seemed to him that three young men came and said, "Arise, Sergius, and say to Abba Daniel: 'The time of your dwelling in the temple is already fulfilled. Come now, and prepare yourself for greater struggles.'" Since Sergius had made the divine vision known to him—for it was known to that man that he should in no way contradict the promptings of the Almighty, and moreover, the second vision agreed with the first, and besides, the garment of Simeon was a pledge of such a way of life—Sergius was sent to examine the hills and the more deserted places, to see where it was fitting to set up a pillar and live the life of quietude which he loved. After he had searched the parts of the hill well, when he came to the spot where the pillar was to stand, You, O God of all, showed this as well. For the aforementioned Sergius immediately saw a white dove, which kept flapping its wings frequently, and then, after flying up a little, would fall back down again. Thinking, therefore, that it was trapped and caught in snares, he hurried toward it and hastened to take it with his hand. But it immediately flew away and was invisible to the eyes. Having thus received a clear sign of the foundation, and having himself also learned how suitable the place was, he went down to the holy man and reported everything in detail, along with the vision he had seen. And he was pleased to hear it and hastened to reach the place. Marcus, a friend of his and otherwise an acquaintance, donated the pillar. And when that which is customarily called the "modius" was ready and had been fitted to the top of the column, he ordered the wall of the temple to be pierced for him around midnight, and thus, escaping the notice of all, he ascended. And then he offered a prayer with joy, which was thus: "Glory be to You, Christ God, that You have deemed even me worthy of such blessings and of such a way of life. But You know, Master, that trusting in You alone I ascend this pillar and undergo this struggle. Accept my purpose, strengthen me for the course, and finish the undertaking." Thus the blessed man was not only heavenly in spirit, but even as far as
his body could allow, he contended to be—and for the rest, he was alone on the column, placing himself on the boundary of heaven and earth. From the latter, indeed, he was withdrawing in both flesh and spirit, while towards the former he was striving.
XVI. But the envious eye could not bear these things, nor were they tolerable to that very father of envy. But when the master of the place—whose name was Gelasius—came to the knowledge of these events, being disturbed in soul by thoughtless anger, or rather moved by a demonic perversity and driven to madness against the holy man (the cause being that he had not set up the pillar with his consent), he reported what had happened to the Emperor, and also reported this to Gennadius, for this man had been appointed Bishop of Constantinople after the passing of Anatolius. Now, he did not get a hearing from the Emperor; but the Bishop, moved by envy, or perhaps yielding to the one who had asked him, thought it right not only for the pillar to be taken down, but for the saint to be subjected to punishment.
Gelasius therefore went up, being full of anger, having taken along many others with him, and desiring to bring the holy man down from the pillar. But the Lord, who is always wonderful in His saints, acted as He now also did, restraining him who was acting with such audacity and glorifying His servant who was hidden. For when there was the greatest serenity, and the air was pure and clear, clouds suddenly gathered, and a thick mist descended, and a violent rain and hail were brought down upon them. And so great was the force of this, and the threat of the wrath from above, that the leaves of the vines even fell off, to say nothing of the fruit, for it was then the time of the vintage. Yet not even these things checked the madness of the deranged man. But though he was close to perishing, not even thus did he learn to be wise, nor did he deign to learn how much Daniel was enriched with grace from above; but panting and drenched, he came on with those about him, indignantly complaining, threatening, shouting, and attempting to cast down the holy man.
XVIII. But after those who were with him suggested what was best, saying that the column was not even near the field, and that from it he would have no trouble at all, but rather that he would enjoy many prayers and that it would be of the greatest use to him to have a good neighbor; after they said these things, they persuaded him. And then he ordered him to descend—moved, I suppose, by shame before those who were present, so that he might not appear to have come in vain—but later promised him the ascent, and that he could remain as long as he wished after having ascended. When, therefore, a ladder had been placed against the column, the holy man was descending, and as he was already setting foot on the sixth step, pity seized the unyielding man, as he saw those feet like other columns, which those all-night and all-day-long stations had rendered swollen, ulcerated, and full of wounds. Having gazed at this with astonishment, he again adjured the holy man to go back up, and asked forgiveness for his undertaking. Later, he also caused the column to be made higher, thicker, and the base...
and everything else more magnificent, and made the emperor more certain of his patience and endurance. And although these things, which are very numerous and great, would suffice to show the life of the holy man and the grace which he had obtained from God, this discourse, considering these things to be small and, as it were, elements and beginnings, hastens to declare those things which are by far greater than these. A XVIII. A short time later, there came to the holy man a certain man, Thracian by race, advanced in age, who earned his living by drafting contracts—what the Greeks call a legal expert. B This man, bringing an only son possessed by an evil spirit, placed him before the column, and tears ran from his eyes, and he added words even more pitiful than the tears. For see how many reasons he had for lamentation: his nature, his gray hair, the youth of his child, the fact that all his hopes hung on this one boy, the fact that he had set out from afar and endured a long journey, and this for a boy who was possessed and behaving in such a disordered and mad fashion. And when finally he added that "This is the thirtieth day that the evil spirit has been crying out your name," he immediately possessed an aptitude for mercy in his own ready soul. And how could the disciple of Christ not have compassion? "If, therefore," he said, "you believe that through me your son will be healed, it shall be done for you according to your faith." And at the same time he ordered him to be given to drink of the oil from the saints that were there. When this was done, the cruel demon immediately threw him to the ground and tormented him with many spasms, then cried out in a loud wail, "I am going out! Alas, I am going out!" When the eighth day had already arrived, since the demon-possessed boy had vomited up black and congealed blood and had been healed by the prayers of the renowned Father, he was handed over, acting in a healthy manner, to his own father. But he, taking his child, used his tongue for thanksgiving and his tears for joy. For there are tears of pleasure, when, after extreme sorrow, the peaks of joy take hold of the soul and immediately dissolve the cloud that was pressing upon it, and they use the natural flow of tears with pleasure. Thus, the boy is not only healed of the evil demon, but is immediately armed against the demons, since after his healing his father consecrated him to God, and prepared him to put on the monastic habit and way of life, and handed him over to the physician of the body to be made also a physician of the soul; who also drew near pure to the Pure, having left behind all the vanity of life. This was one such instance, and it is not at all inferior to the one that follows.
XIX. A certain Cyrus, who had previously been a governor and afterwards ascended to an episcopal see in Phrygia—it was Cotyaeum—and had departed from it because of some slander stirred up against him, had a daughter, Alexandria by name. She had suffered the same things as the one mentioned before, and was being severely troubled by a demon. Her father, therefore, taking the holy man to mind, brought the child to him with the natural warmth of affection
mingled with another—namely, that of faith. As soon, then, as the holy man's hands were laid upon her head, the spirit was put to flight, and the sufferer was immediately seen to be whole. And indeed, to such a miracle another was joined. For Cyrus, now having great confidence in the holy man, was making use of his grace. Hence, he also brought to him the wife of the man to whom he had entrusted the care of his fields, for she too was possessed by a demon; and his confidence did not deceive him, but she, too, along with the daughter, was cleansed from the madness of the demon. For all of which he rendered him great thanks (for he was a learned man and had been educated in humane letters), and later he inscribed an epigram upon the column erected for him, which runs as follows: There stands a man, assailed from every side, fearing no winds; he is fed by ambrosial food and bloodless thirst, having struck his roots into a dual column. And Simeon is the root, preaching the Son of a Mother who knew not wedlock.
XX. Furthermore, there was a day, the most auspicious of all days. A certain man, from among those who lived near the Euxine Sea, came bringing his son, who was himself possessed by a demon, and besides that was deaf and dumb, such as the Gospels teach us Christ healed. When they were already approaching the pillar, the son with his father, the demon shook the boy, and tearing him away from those who were holding him, cast him toward the ladder which at that time happened to be leaning against the pillar. And he immediately climbed the ladder, while the holy man, looking down from above, prayed to God. For how, indeed, would he not have been dashed to pieces by the demon when thrown from the ladder, had not the other restrained the demon with his prayer? He did not, therefore, lay his hands upon him, nor did he do what he was wont to do for others, but he remained above praying, and the boy, while still below and not yet even having reached the middle of the ladder, attained healing. Having mentioned only one, I will recall the others in their own time.
XXI. Leo the Great ruled the Roman Empire, but he could not master nature, for he lacked male offspring. What does he do? He takes refuge in God, the Lord of all, using Daniel as an intercessor with Him. He obeyed, and immediately signaled that his wife would give birth in the following year. The matter, therefore, succeeded just as he had said, and nature yielded to the prayers—not only to grant a son, but to grant him at the very time the holy, god-breathed mouth had foretold. Thus he was not only a son of prayer, but he also gave to others to have sons through prayer. Wherefore, rewarding him, the emperor laid for him the foundation of a third column. D
XXII. Since, therefore, fame declared the holy man everywhere, the Empress Eudocia, who had recently been saved from Africa, came to him; and, paying him great reverence and bestowing much honor upon him...
begged requesting that he descend from the column, come to her own estates, and dwell there. "For I have many [workers] there," she said, "who are at once friends of silence and able to offer other comfort." He, however, accepting her good intentions and praising her, did not judge it right to move, but rather to persevere where he had been planted by Christ to take root in virtue and bear fruit in the proper season. Having said these things and others like them, and having deemed her worthy of a blessing, he sent her away. And then he moves to the column of Gelasius; and, together with the column (for it was loftier), his virtue was also increased, and he took up a higher way of life.
Yet these things were not to be bearable for the devil, nor for his followers. Therefore, he himself, the one who had kindled the furnace of temptations for him again, made use of those among the heretics in Byzantium as his ministers. Having taken up a certain woman from among the prostitutes, who was Asian by birth, and having sailed to Byzantium—where she, alas! made an evil trade of her own body—they deceived her with words and persuaded her with oaths and the promise of money to do what they desired. They spurred her on, prepared her to go to the holy man, and to instill a longing in either him or one of his disciples, and to lure them into an improper desire. The licentious one therefore approaches the chaste one; the impure soul approaches the pure one, bringing with her a multitude of followers, using disgusting illusions and masks and clothed in gold, with the trappings of other adornments, thinking to steal away and entice those eyes which had loved nothing earthly, nothing composed of matter. And staying in one of the fields which was opposite the column and the sheepfold of the rational flock of this good shepherd, Daniel, she pretended to be ill, so that, if possible, she might be the cause of spiritual sickness to the holy man himself; or, if not that, at least bring ruin upon his disciples.
XXIII. But when, after having remained for a long time, all her craft proved vain, she goes down to those who had sent her, and the execrable one speaks and utters false charges against the holy man—things which had never even come to the mind of that great man (for the one who had so thoroughly shaken off [carnal] thoughts did not believe it, nor did any of his disciples, far from it). These were the charges: "Daniel," she said, "had been thoroughly captivated by love for me; he had received the dart in his soul, and finally he instructed his disciples to bring me up to him via the ladder; but because I would not submit, they plotted to destroy me, that this intemperance might be concealed; and behold, with difficulty I escaped their hands and have been saved by flight." Thus, while that maenad was being proven to be a liar, those men snatched up her words, paying heed to them as if they were most true; for what they wished, those things they believed to be true, and passed them on to others. But God was the same here also, and the justice that is inescapable and unerring. What happens then, and how are these things ordered? An evil spirit is immediately sent against the base and lying woman...
and it compels the unwilling woman to utter the fraud of the heretics within her, and her own lie and device once again. The citizens therefore took her, and leading her to the holy man, they begged him to heal her. But he, the true disciple of Christ, who said that one must forgive the debts of one's brothers seventy times seven, and love one's enemies, fulfilled both commands at once with one action: the one by forgiving the injustice, and the other by commiserating with the calamity, or rather, by perfectly restoring her through the use of prayer. And having anointed her with the oil of the saints as if with some healing medicine, he immediately cast out the evil spirit like one of the venomous things, and he handed the sufferer over to her own people, sound. She, embracing his pillar, gave great thanks and subsequently made many promises to God, as one who had escaped a double disease—not only that which appeared and brought harm to the object that was seen, but much more the invisible one which was self-willed and could punish the immortal soul immortally.
XXVI. The blessed man was able to heal not only the disease that had just fallen upon her, but, like a good doctor, he was able both to know the future and to see and speak correctly by prophecy about those things that were to happen. Having received the revelation from above that great threats were about to attack the great city, he informed the bishop Gennadius, commanding him to use more intense prayer. Indeed, he also predicted similar things to Emperor Leo, and he ordered public processions to take place twice a week; for thus, he assured them, the wrath of God would become milder. These men, however, having held the commands of the holy man in contempt, were about to learn by experience those things which the multitude of their sins had caused to come to pass. The discourse as it proceeds will make this clear. But since it was necessary that Daniel should also be the sacrificer of the divine Lamb, as he had already sacrificed both his body and soul to Him, the Spirit moves the emperor from above, and the emperor also moves Gennadius, and he writes to him, that taking many from the Church, he should go up to the great Daniel and complete him with the order of the priesthood. When he had done these things, he who had great power to foresee the future first perceived in his spirit the coming to him. Then, when he had already arrived at the pillar, he said that he had long desired to ascend to him, but that ecclesiastical cares had prevented him from fulfilling his desire, yet now he had come both to see him and to enjoy his prayers and words. He requested that a ladder also be brought. "In vain," said Daniel, who was endowed with great power of prediction to him, "did the one who moved you to come to us cause you such labor." This brought both astonishment and fear to the priest—astonishment at how he had discerned the manner of his arrival in this way, and fear because the thoughts of his own mind were manifest to the great man; for, had he not been compelled by the one in command, he would never have agreed to ascend.
